\gamma^{2}=E_{p}^{\rm lab}/E_{e}^{\rm lab}\neq 1
and a lab frame rapidity cut
\eta_{0}^{\rm lab}
that distinguishes the regions
X
and
Y
, which we discuss in detail in
section
3.3
.

Let us derive the scaling of the
\lambda_{i}
’s (and in turn the scaling of the Lorentz invariants in
section
2.1
) from the ground up. Our first step is to note that we do not need to assign a scaling to the invariants
y
and
\bar{x}
, as they only appear in cross section coefficients
c_{i}
, not in the structure functions
F_{i}^{D}
that contain the QCD dynamics we will factorize.
To prevent superfluous factors of
\bar{x}
and
y
from appearing in our analysis,
we work in the Breit frame.
10
10

10

This can be contrasted with the analysis of small-
x
inclusive DIS in ref.
[
189
]
, which was carried out in the
ep
CM frame. There, it was convenient to assign a scaling
y\sim x\sim\lambda
because
y
appeared in the components
(q^{+},q^{-},q_{\perp})
in this frame, even though the final results were independent of this choice.

Next, we impose the forward scattering constraint
x\ll 1
in eq. (
2.67
). Using
xy=Q^{2}/s=\lambda^{2}
, and the fact that we do not assign a scaling to
y
(effectively
y\sim 1
), we have

\displaystyle x\sim\lambda^{2}\ll 1\,.

(3.18)

Combining this with
Q=\sqrt{sxy}\sim\lambda\,\sqrt{s}
and using eq. (
2.1
) implies

\displaystyle W^{2}\sim s\,,

(3.19)

indicating that both
s
and
W^{2}
correspond to the hardest scale in (quasi-)diffraction.
The other forward scattering condition
-t\ll W^{2}
in eq. (
2.67
) implies that

\displaystyle\frac{(-t)}{W^{2}}\sim\frac{(-t)}{s}=\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}\ll 1\,,

(3.20)

which is trivially satisfied when
\lambda_{t}\lesssim 1
, but is a nontrivial constraint in diffractive events with
\lambda_{t}\gg 1
(see e.g. the HERA measurements refs.
[
190
,
191
,
192
,
193
,
194
,
195
,
196
]
).

Next, we examine the implications of the small invariant mass conditions in eq. (
2.63
),
m_{X}^{2},m_{Y}^{2}\ll W^{2}\sim s
. Comparing the Lorentz invariants from eqs. (
2.1
) and (
2.1
) with the definition of our power counting parameters, we find that

\displaystyle\frac{m_{Y}^{2}}{s}=\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}\rho^{2}\ll 1\,,

\displaystyle z\sim\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}(1+\rho^{2})\ll 1\,,

(3.21)

where we have used eq. (
3.20
). Finally, we use that
m_{X}^{2}/W^{2}=\frac{x(1-\beta)}{\beta(1-x)}+t/W^{2}\ll 1
implies that

x\ll\beta/(1-\beta)
, and thus

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\sim x\ll\beta\,,

(3.22)

which holds for both
\beta\sim\lambda
and
\beta\sim 1
. (We do not consider more exotic
scalings for
\beta
, like
\beta\sim\lambda^{3/2}
.)
The kinematic restriction
m_{X}^{2}\geq 0
implies an upper bound on
\lambda_{t}
given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{t}^{2}\leq(1-\beta)/\beta\,.

(3.23)

For
\beta\sim 1
, this bounds
\lambda_{t}\lesssim 1
, implying that the forward scattering constraint in eq. (
3.18
) implies the one in eq. (
3.20
).
We could have a large
\lambda_{t}\gg 1
while still ensuring
\lambda_{t}\ll\lambda^{-1}
if we choose a small
\beta\sim\lambda^{2}\ll 1
or
\beta\sim\lambda_{t}^{-2}\ll 1
.
For example, we could have
\lambda_{t}^{2}\sim\lambda^{-1}\gg 1
and still satisfy eq. (
3.20
), which in this case is an independent condition.
The
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
case is most commonly studied in the diffraction theory literature, likely due to its relationship to hard-collinear factorization; however, diffraction does not require
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
.

We also consider how the lower bounds in eq. (
2.63
),
\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}\lesssim m_{X}^{2},m_{Y}^{2}
, constrain
\lambda_{\Lambda}
. When
X
is a jet, the condition
\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}\ll m_{X}^{2}
gives
\lambda_{\Lambda}^{2}\ll(1-\beta)/\beta-\lambda_{t}^{2}
. In contrast, if
X
is a single particle with
m_{X}^{2}\sim\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}
, then

\lambda_{\Lambda}^{2}\sim(1-\beta)/\beta-\lambda_{t}^{2}
.
Likewise, when
Y
is a multi-particle state (incoherent diffraction), we have
\lambda_{\Lambda}^{2}\ll\rho^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}
. When
Y
is a proton (coherent diffraction), we have

\lambda_{\Lambda}^{2}\sim\rho^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}
.

Note that these power counting results tell us that in the (quasi-)diffractive region, the hadronic system and central jet are always well separated by a rapidity gap. In particular, since
z\ll 1
and
\bar{x}\sim x\ll 1
from eq. (
2.14
), then the rapidity gap condition in eq. (
2.65
) in the lab frame becomes

\displaystyle x\Big(\frac{\bar{x}}{x}-y\Big)\quad\ll\quad\frac{1}{z}\,,

(3.24)

which is satisfied.
Applying instead the Breit frame results in eq. (
2.1.2
) to the calculation of rapidities, we see that

\displaystyle\frac{p_{X}^{-}}{p_{X}^{+}}\sim\frac{1-\beta}{\beta}\ll\frac{1}{x}\ll\frac{p^{\prime-}}{p^{\prime+}}\sim\frac{1}{xz}\,,

(3.25)

which is also satisfied.
That is, we have rapidity gaps in both the Breit and the lab frames.
We discuss the exact relation between rapidity gaps in the lab and Breit frames further in
appendix
A.1.3
.

Finally, we revisit our discussion from
section
2.3
and examine how the power counting parameters in eq. (
3.17
) behave in gapped hard scattering. In this case, we power expand in
\lambda_{t}\ll 1

and choose
Q^{2}\sim W^{2}\sim s
, which implies that
\lambda\sim 1
and
x\sim 1
.
Additionally, this gapped hard scattering has two collimated jets (or hadrons)
X
and
Y
. Therefore we have
m_{Y}^{2}/Q^{2}=\lambda_{t}^{2}\rho^{2}\ll 1
and thus
z\ll 1
. However, the other collimated jet condition gives
m_{X}^{2}/Q^{2}=(1-\beta)/\beta+t/Q^{2}\ll 1
and so here
\beta
must be close to one,
(1-\beta)\ll 1
. Nonetheless, the hard scattering Lorentz invariants still satisfy the rapidity gap condition between jets in eq. (
2.65
). In summary, the key distinction between gapped hard scattering and (quasi-)diffraction is that the former has
\lambda\sim 1
and the latter has
\lambda\ll 1
.

3.2.1
Scaling of momenta in the Breit frame

Let us examine how momenta scale in (quasi-)diffraction.
Our focus will be on the scaling in
\lambda
and
\lambda_{t}
, though we will initially also track dependence on
\rho
and
\beta
.
To simplify our analysis, we take
\lambda_{\Lambda}\sim 1
, and thus do not initially try to distinguish the scales
-t
and
\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}
. We can do so without loss of generality, as later on we will see that it is straightforward to consider further expansions in
\lambda_{\Lambda}
.
Likewise, we postpone discussion of the
\lambda_{g}
expansion until the next section.
Writing eqs. (
2.8
) and (
2.1.2
) in terms of the power counting parameters in eq. (
3.17
), we find

\displaystyle k,\,

\displaystyle k^{\prime}\sim\sqrt{s}(\lambda,\lambda,\lambda)\,,

\displaystyle p\sim\sqrt{s}(0,\lambda^{-1},0)\,,

(3.26)

\displaystyle q\sim\sqrt{s}(\lambda,\,\lambda,\,0)\,,

\displaystyle p^{\prime}\sim\sqrt{s}\Big(\lambda^{3}\lambda_{t}^{2}(1+\rho^{2}),\lambda^{-1},\lambda\lambda_{t}\Big)\,,

\displaystyle p_{X}\sim\sqrt{s}(\lambda,\,\lambda/\beta,\,\lambda\lambda_{t})\,,

\displaystyle\tau\sim\sqrt{s}\Big(\lambda^{3}\lambda_{t}^{2}(1+\rho^{2}),\lambda/\beta,\lambda\lambda_{t}\Big)\,.

Although we have kept factors of
\beta
in eq. (
3.26
) for completeness, we henceforth simply take
\beta\sim 1
. It is clear that the modes in eq. (
3.26
) bear close resemblance to the SCET modes in
table
1
. In particular, we see that
q
and
p_{X}
have soft scaling,
p
and
p^{\prime}
have
n
-collinear scaling with
p_{i}^{+}\ll p_{i}^{\perp}\ll p_{i}^{-}
,
and
\tau
has Glauber momentum scaling with
\tau^{+}\tau^{-}\ll\vec{\tau}_{\perp}^{\,2}
. Thus, Glauber SCET is the appropriate EFT for analyzing diffraction, and we can describe each particle or jet in terms of its corresponding field in
section
3.1.1
.
Due to our frame choice we have a non-standard scaling for collinear momenta. This leads to a modified scaling for the
n
-collinear fields and building blocks, as shown in
table
4
. The soft fields still follow the counting shown in
table
2
.

SCET field

(n\cdot p,\bar{n}\cdot p,|\vec{p}_{\perp}|)

\xi_{n}

(\lambda^{3},\lambda^{-1},\lambda)

A_{n}

(\lambda^{3},\lambda^{-1},\lambda)

Building block

Scaling

\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{\mu}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n\perp}^{\mu A}

\lambda

\chi_{n}

\lambda^{1/2}

Table 4
:
Field and building block
\lambda
-scalings for the
n
-collinear modes in eq. (
3.26
). These are modified from tables
2
and
3
due to our frame choice here.

Since we have Glauber exchange between soft and
n
-collinear fields, the relevant operators are those in eq. (
3.16
).
The derivation of a factorized (quasi-)diffractive cross section forms the focus of
section
4
.
Additionally, we remark that as we alluded in
section
2.2
, it is now apparent that in diffraction, the
p_{i}^{+}
and
p_{i}^{\perp}
lightcone components of
p
and
p^{\prime}
are not multiples of one another, implying that the assumption
p^{\prime}=(1-\xi)p
does not hold, which will become relevant in the full leading-power factorized description of diffraction.

It is straightforward to find the
(+,-,\perp)
scaling in
\lambda
for
U^{\mu}
and
X^{\mu}
in eq. (
2.40
),

\displaystyle U^{\mu}

\displaystyle\sim(1,1,0)\,,

\displaystyle X^{\mu}

\displaystyle\sim(0,0,1)\,.

(3.27)

If we examine the diffractive cross section in eq. (
2.50
) and compare the structure function prefactors
c_{i}
to one another, we see that they are all the same size with respect to the hadronic power counting,
c_{i}/c_{2}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda^{0})
, because
y\sim 1
and
k\cdot U\sim k\cdot X\sim Q\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda
.
We also observed this when plotting
c_{i}
in
figure
3
. We note that the hadronic structure functions
F_{i}^{D}(x,Q^{2},\beta,t,m_{Y}^{2})
are independent of
\bar{x}
and
y
. Thus, when we carry out an expansion of the structure functions using Glauber SCET, we can apply our results to any value of
y
(e.g.
y=10^{-3}
or
y=1/2
) and any value of
\bar{x}
, within their physical bounds.
In contrast, working out the scaling of the
F_{i}^{D}
’s themselves is a dynamical question, which we can only answer after carrying out their factorization.

3.3
Ultrasoft radiation in the gap

Next, we must add to our formalism a description of softer radiation that is below the detection threshold in the gapped region.
This radiation will affect theoretical predictions, particularly for quasi-diffraction.
Up until this point, we have focused on a hadronic system
Y
with total momentum
p^{\prime}
and a central system
X
with total momentum
p_{X}
. These systems contain forward and soft particles, respectively, but also can include low-energy particles, with momenta denoted by
p_{uc}
,
that contribute to both the gapped and non-gapped regions,
as shown in
figure
6
.
We use
p_{n}^{\prime}
to refer to the
n
-collinear radiation in
Y(p^{\prime})
that does not enter the gap, and
p_{X_{s}}
to refer to the soft radiation in
X(p_{X})
that does not enter the gap. These scale in the Breit frame as:

\displaystyle p_{X_{s}}\sim\sqrt{s}(\lambda,\,\lambda/\beta,\,\lambda\lambda_{t})\,,

\displaystyle p^{\prime}_{n}\sim\sqrt{s}\Big(\lambda^{3}\lambda_{t}^{2}(1+\rho^{2}),\lambda^{-1},\lambda\lambda_{t}\Big)\,.

(3.28)

Note that the overall scalings for
p_{X}
and
p^{\prime}
in eq. (
3.26
) remain correct.

Figure 6
:
Picture showing a typical quantum field theory amplitude with multiple Glauber exchange, with soft,
n
-collinear, and ultra-collinear (uc) radiation into hadronic states
X
,
Y
, and/or the gap.

The radiation with momentum
p_{uc}
, on the other hand, is most straightforward to analyze in the
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-collinear frame, which is defined by the choice

\displaystyle\bar{q}^{\mu}_{\gamma_{\bar{n}}\text{-frame}}=\sqrt{ys}\,\frac{\bar{n}^{\mu}}{2}-\frac{Q^{2}}{\sqrt{ys}}\,\frac{n^{\mu}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{p}^{\mu}_{\gamma_{\bar{n}}\text{-frame}}=\sqrt{ys}\,\frac{n^{\mu}}{2}\,.

(3.29)

This frame is related to the Breit frame in eq. (
2.1.2
) by a simple boost along the
z
-axis, with

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{i,\gamma_{\bar{n}}\text{-frame}}^{+}=\frac{p_{i,\text{Breit}}^{+}}{\sqrt{x}}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{i,\gamma_{\bar{n}}\text{-frame}}^{-}=\sqrt{x}\,p_{i,\text{Breit}}^{-}\,.

(3.30)

Below we adopt a more compact notation: we drop the subscripts “Breit” and “
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-frame” above,
but put bars over momenta to indicate the
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-collinear frame.
In
appendix
A.1.3
, we give further detail on the relationship between the Breit and lab frames; note that we explicitly include the subscript “lab” when referring to quantities in the lab frame.

The key attribute of the
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-collinear frame is that
X
and
Y

have large momentum components that are parametrically the same size, with
\bar{p}_{X_{s}}^{+}\sim\bar{p}_{n}^{\,\prime-}\sim\sqrt{ys}\sim\lambda^{0}
.
Eq. (
3.30
) implies that in terms of the power counting parameters in eq. (
3.17
), modes in the
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-collinear frame scale as follows (setting
\rho\sim 1
for simplicity):

\bar{q}\sim\sqrt{ys}(1,\lambda^{2},0)
is
\bar{n}
-collinear,
and so is
\bar{p}_{X_{s}}\sim\sqrt{ys}(1,\lambda^{2}/\beta,\lambda\lambda_{t})
.
We still have Glauber scaling for
\bar{\tau}\sim\sqrt{ys}(\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2},\lambda^{2}/\beta,\lambda\lambda_{t})
.
The proton
p
is
n
-collinear with
\bar{p}\sim\sqrt{ys}(0,1,0)
, as is

\bar{p}^{\,\prime}_{n}\sim\sqrt{ys}(\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2},1,\lambda\lambda_{t})
.
These mode identifications hold true for any
\lambda_{t}
, as
\lambda\lambda_{t}\ll 1
. In this frame, the relevant hard scale is
\sqrt{ys}
.
The modes all scale proportionally to
\sqrt{y}
, and we again do not need to assign a power counting to
y
itself.
11
11

11

Note that in the
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-collinear frame, we could also factorize this process using the
n
-
\bar{n}
Glauber operators in eq. (
3.13
); see also ref.
[
51
]
. In this case the restriction on radiation in the gap (which allows for ultrasoft but not soft radiation) enables us to replace
\mathcal{O}_{s}^{AB}\propto\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{2}\delta^{AB}
, so that these operators reduce to an identical form as eq. (
3.12
), just with a renaming of the fields. Thus, such an analysis does not differ from the above. Nevertheless, we find it more intuitive to use the Breit frame.

In the
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-collinear frame,
X
and
Y
are back-to-back, and the only relevant low-energy radiation that populates the gap is that which can modify the masses
m_{X}
and
m_{Y}
. Taking this radiation to have momentum
\bar{p}_{uc}^{\mu}
with
\bar{p}_{uc}^{+}\ll\bar{p}_{X_{s}}^{+}
and
\bar{p}_{uc}^{-}\ll\bar{p}_{n}^{\prime-}
, we have
m_{X}^{2}\sim(\bar{p}_{X_{s}}+\bar{p}_{uc})^{2}\sim\bar{p}_{X_{s}}^{+}\bar{p}_{uc}^{-}\sim\lambda^{2}/\beta
and
m_{Y}^{2}\sim(\bar{p}_{n}^{\prime\,2}+\bar{p}_{uc})^{2}\sim\bar{p}_{n}^{\prime\,-}\bar{p}_{uc}^{+}\sim\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}\rho^{2}
,
so
\bar{p}_{uc}
is isotropic in its
\lambda
scaling, with
\bar{p}_{uc}^{-}\sim\bar{p}_{uc}^{+}\sim\lambda^{2}
.
Thus, we take this radiation to be ultrasoft for its
\lambda
scaling (and collinear for its
\lambda_{t}
scaling)

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{uc}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\sim\sqrt{ys}\bigl(\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2},\lambda^{2},\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}\bigr)\,.

(3.31)

We can use eq. (
3.30
) to transform our
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-frame results back to the Breit frame discussed in
section
2.1.2
.
In the Breit frame, radiation with momentum
p_{uc}^{\mu}
carries an overall ultrasoft scaling
\lambda^{2}
, multiplied by a collinear boost
\sim\lambda
.
We refer to this as ultrasoft-collinear (uc) scaling:

\displaystyle p_{uc}^{\mu}\sim\sqrt{ys}\>\big(\lambda^{3}\lambda_{t}^{2},\lambda,\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}\big)\,,

(3.32)

where the collinear
\lambda
scaling arises from
\sqrt{x}\sim\lambda
. We illustrate how these modes look in the Breit and
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
frames in
figure
7
.
The leading-power interactions among uc fermions and uc gluons are described by an ultrasoft Lagrangian expressed as a function of uc fields, i.e. simply replacing
{\cal L}_{us}^{(0)}[\psi_{us},A_{us}]\to{\cal L}_{uc}^{(0)}[\psi_{uc},A_{uc}]
in eq. (
3.5
).

(a)                      (b)

Figure 7
:

Comparison of radiation in (a) the
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-frame and (b) the Breit frame using the power counting in
\lambda
. The
n
- and
\bar{n}
-collinear directions points towards the positive and negative horizontal axes, respectively. The dashed boundary in the transverse plane separates two hemispheres.
In both frames, a rapidity cut (black cone) separates particles belonging to the hadronic system
Y
(inside cone) and the central system
X
(outside cone). In the Breit frame, the system
Y
contains forward hadronic particles
p_{n}^{\prime}
(blue), and the system
X
contains central soft particles
p_{s}
(green). Both
X
and
Y
can contain ultracollinear (uc) particles
p_{uc}
(yellow) that can populate the gapped region below the experimental detection threshold.

Next, we explore how our description of gap radiation relates to experimental methods for identifying diffractive processes.

Small
m_{X}
method.

Experimentally, it is natural to distinguish the regions
X
and
Y
by a rapidity cut
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
in the lab frame, taking
p_{i}^{\rm lab}\in X
when
\eta_{i}^{\rm lab}<\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
, and
p_{i}^{\rm lab}\in Y
when
\eta_{i}^{\rm lab}>\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
. This closely resembles the
m_{X}
-cut method for identifying diffractive processes discussed in
section
2.3
, as the gap is only imposed indirectly by the measurement of a small
m_{X}
.
Theoretically, it is more natural to define
X
and
Y
by a rapidity cut
{\bar{\eta}_{0}}
in the
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-frame.
Here, we demonstrate that these two definitions are identical to quite high accuracy.
First, we remark that
since we are considering low-mass hadronic particles here, their pseudo-rapidity and rapidity are approximately equal,
\eta_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\ln(p_{i}^{-}/p_{i}^{+})
.
With a rapidity cut in the
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-frame we
have

\displaystyle\bar{p}_{i}\in X\text{\ \ when\ \ }\bar{\eta}_{i}<{\bar{\eta}_{0}}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\text{and\qquad}\bar{p}_{i}\in Y\text{\ \ when\ \ }\bar{\eta}_{i}>{\bar{\eta}_{0}}\,.

(3.33)

From eq. (
3.30
), we see that the boundary between
X
and
Y
in the Breit frame is given by

\displaystyle\eta_{0}={\bar{\eta}_{0}}-\frac{1}{2}\ln x\,,\qquad\text{with\quad}X:\ \eta_{i}<\eta_{0}\quad\text{and\quad}Y:\ \eta_{i}>\eta_{0}\,,

(3.34)

as shown in
figure
7
. Likewise, we use the results of
appendix
A.1.3
to express the boundary between
X
and
Y
in the lab frame as

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma^{2}}{y}e^{2{\bar{\eta}_{0}}}=e^{2\eta_{0}^{\rm lab}}-2\sqrt{\frac{x\gamma^{2}(1-y)}{y}}\cos\bigl(\phi^{\rm lab}\bigr)\>e^{\eta_{0}^{\rm lab}}+\frac{x\gamma^{2}(1-y)}{y}\,,

(3.35)

where
\gamma^{2}=E_{p}/E_{e}
, the ratio of proton and electron lab frame energies, and

\displaystyle X:\ \eta_{i}^{\rm lab}<\eta_{0}^{\rm lab}\bigl(\phi_{i}^{\rm lab}\bigr)\qquad\text{and }\qquad Y:\ \eta_{i}^{\rm lab}>\eta_{0}^{\rm lab}\bigl(\phi_{i}^{\rm lab}\bigr)\,.

(3.36)

Unlike the simple lab-frame rapidity cut
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
that is natural for experiments, we find that the theoretical cut
\eta_{0}^{\rm lab}
is a nontrivial function of the azimuthal angle
\phi^{\rm lab}
and the quantity
x\gamma^{2}(1-y)/y=xE_{p}(1-y)/(yE_{e})
.
For diffraction at HERA and the EIC, we typically have that
E_{p}/E_{e}
is large,
x
is small,
and the rapidity cut is large and lies
in the forward region, e.g.
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}>5
(or
{\bar{\eta}_{0}}>5
and
\eta_{0}^{\rm lab}>5
).
For sufficiently large
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
,
the last two terms in eq. (
3.35
) are small and subleading.
That is, if we choose

\displaystyle{\bar{\eta}_{0}}=\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}+\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{y}{\gamma^{2}}\qquad\longrightarrow\qquad\eta_{0}^{\rm lab}\simeq\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}\,.

(3.37)

That is, this choice of
{\bar{\eta}_{0}}
causes the experimentally- and theoretically-motivated lab frame rapidity bounds to match to very good approximation.
In
figure
8
, we compare
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
and the
\eta_{0}^{\rm lab}
from eq. (
3.35
) with
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}=3
or
4
, demonstrating that
these definitions agree to better than 5% or even to better than 1%, depending on the choice of kinematics.
At smaller
E_{p}/E_{e}
and
x
, or larger
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
, the agreement improves further.
Thus, the factorization we derive in this work, using a cut
\eta_{0}=\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}+\frac{1}{2}\ln[y/(x\gamma^{2})]
, is directly applicable for data
that separates
X
and
Y
by measuring a small
m_{X}
in a region defined up to some forward rapidity cut.

Figure 8
:

Plot of
(\eta_{0}^{\rm lab}-\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab})/\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
to compare the rapidity boundaries that define
X
and
Y
in the lab frame (
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
) and the
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-frame (
{\bar{\eta}_{0}}
converted to a lab frame
\eta_{0}^{\rm lab}
). For HERA kinematics we take
E_{p}=920\,{\rm GeV}
and
E_{e}=27.6\,{\rm GeV}
(left panel), while for EIC kinematics we choose
E_{p}=41\,{\rm GeV}
and
E_{e}=10\,{\rm GeV}
in order to minimize the ratio (right panel).

For future purposes, it is useful to define a shorthand notation for the combination of factors

\displaystyle h=e^{-{\bar{\eta}_{0}}}=\frac{\gamma}{\sqrt{y}}\,e^{-\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}}\,\sim\,\lambda_{g}\,.

(3.38)

The restriction on rapidities in eq. (
2.66
) that constrains the
n
-collinear particles to be well within
Y
becomes

\displaystyle z\ll h^{2}\,,\ \ \text{or alternatively}\ \ \lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}\ll\lambda_{g}^{2}\,.

(3.39)

This means that for a given
m_{Y}^{2}-t
, there is a restriction on the choice of
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
. It is useful to write this constraint as

\displaystyle m_{Y}^{2}-t\ll\frac{h^{2}Q^{2}}{x}=s\gamma^{2}e^{-2\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}}\,.

(3.40)

Intuitively, this is due to the fact that for larger
-t
, the forward-scattered
n
-collinear hadronic system has a larger transverse kick. This requires increasing the size of
Y
and thus taking smaller values of
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
.
To satisfy this constraint while still leaving an appreciable range for
-t
, one can consider using smaller values of
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
than those used in past HERA analyses. For example, with HERA kinematics the choices
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}=\{3,4,5,6\}
give the bounds
\sqrt{m_{Y}^{2}-t}\ll\{92,34,12,4.6\}
GeV, respectively. For a large
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}=6
we are thus forced to work in a nonperturbative region where
m_{Y}^{2}-t\sim\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}
, while for smaller
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
there can still be an appreciable perturbative region for
m_{Y}^{2}-t
.
The values
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}=3,4
were used in
figure
8
, which increases the allowed values of
m_{Y}^{2}-t
. This comes at the expense of introducing
\phi
-dependence in the rapidity boundary from the translation between the lab and Breit frames; these are at the level of other power-suppressed contributions, and hence can be ignored.

We can also consider a constraint that ensures that the soft particles are fully in
X
. Since
p_{uc}^{+}\ll p_{X_{s}}^{+}
, this constraint can be imposed by demanding
p_{X}^{-}/p_{X}^{+}\ll e^{2\eta_{0}}
, which we can write as
xh^{2}\ll\beta/(1-\beta)
. With
\beta\sim 1
, we can express this more simply as

\displaystyle xh^{2}\ll 1\,,\ \ \text{or alternatively,}\ \ \lambda^{2}\lambda_{g}^{2}\ll 1\,.

(3.41)

Rapidity boundary in the Breit frame.

If experimentally the rapidity cut defining
X
and
Y
were instead imposed in the Breit frame,
through
h_{0}\equiv e^{-\eta_{0}}
, then the condition in eq. (
3.40
) becomes

\displaystyle xz\ll h_{0}^{2}\qquad\text{as}\qquad m_{Y}^{2}-t\ll\frac{h_{0}^{2}Q^{2}}{x^{2}}\,.

(3.42)

Although this is more challenging experimentally and leads to a joint bound on
x^{2}(m_{Y}^{2}-t)
, it does not rely on the approximation between rapidity boundaries shown in
figure
8
.

Rapidity gap method.

A second way to define
X
and
Y
is the rapidity gap method from
section
2.3
, which requires a gapped region
S:\eta_{\rm min}^{\rm lab}<\eta_{i}^{\rm lab}<\eta_{\rm max}^{\rm lab}
in the lab frame, with a detection threshold
E_{i}^{\rm lab}<E_{\rm gap}^{\rm lab}
. Here,
Y
consists of particles with rapidity
\eta_{i}^{\rm lab}>\eta_{\rm max}^{\rm lab}
, so we have
S\subset X
. Transforming this constraint to a Breit frame built from an incoming lepton with momentum in the
x
-
z
plane, we find

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{x\gamma^{2}}{y}}p_{i}^{-}+\sqrt{\frac{y}{x\gamma^{2}}}\Big(1+\frac{x\gamma^{2}(1-y)}{y}\Big)p_{i}^{+}-2\sqrt{\frac{x\gamma^{2}(1-y)}{y}}|\vec{p}_{i\perp}|\cos\phi_{i}<2E_{\rm gap}^{\rm lab}\,.

(3.43)

Due to the hierarchy in uc momentum components in eq. (
3.32
), if
x\gamma^{2}\sim 1
then eq. (
3.43
) gives the constraint
\sqrt{x\gamma^{2}/y}\,p_{uc\,i}^{-}<2E_{\rm gap}^{\rm lab}
, whereas if
x\gamma^{2}\sim x
then eq. (
3.43
) gives
\sqrt{x\gamma^{2}/y}\,p_{uc\,i}^{-}+\sqrt{y/(x\gamma^{2})}\,p_{uc\,i}^{+}<2E_{\rm gap}^{\rm lab}
.
Therefore, the uc radiation is sensitive to three regions: region
X/S
and region
Y
, corresponding to uc radiation contributing to measurements of
m_{X}
and
m_{Y}
, respectively, as well as the gapped region
S\subset X
, which is sensitive to
E_{\rm gap}^{\rm lab}
.
Thus, the rapidity gap method introduces sensitivity to an additional scale
E_{\rm gap}^{\rm lab}
, which is typically taken to be
\lesssim 1\,{\rm GeV}
. The full treatment of this additional scale leads to a more complicated factorization that can involve additional expansions and modes. In
section
4
, we briefly comment on this case but do not carry out a full analysis.

Coherent forward detector method.

Diffractive processes can also be identified by the presence of a single proton in the forward detector. In this case, the division between
X
and
Y
can be taken at the boundary of the forward detector,
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}\simeq 6
, similar to the small-
m_{X}
method. Thus, the factorization we derive below is applicable to this method.

SCET operators for gap radiation.

To discuss how uc modes enter into Glauber SCET, let us denote the uc radiation state with momentum
p_{uc}
as
Z_{uc}
, and let us analyze the case where
p_{X}^{-}\sim p_{uc}^{-}
and
p^{\prime\,+}\sim p_{uc}^{+}
.
(It is straightforward to make further expansions to obtain results for
p_{X}^{-}\gg p_{uc}^{-}
and/or
p^{\prime\,+}\gg p_{uc}^{+}
.)
By comparing which momentum components are the same order in the power counting,
the scaling of
p_{uc}^{\mu}
implies that at leading order in the power expansion, only
p_{uc}^{+}\sim p_{n}^{+}
can influence the momentum of
Y
, and only
p_{uc}^{-}\sim p_{X_{s}}^{-}\sim\tau^{-}

can influence the momentum of
X
. In particular, we can decompose

\displaystyle p^{\prime}=p_{n}^{\prime}+p_{uc}^{Y}\,,\qquad\quad p_{X}=p_{X_{s}}+p_{uc}^{X}\,.

(3.44)

To leading power,
p^{\prime-}=p_{n}^{\prime-}
,
p_{\perp}^{\prime}=p_{n\perp}^{\prime}
,
p_{X}^{+}=p_{X_{s}}^{+}
, and
p_{X}^{\perp}=p_{X_{s}}^{\perp}
, but we must be careful to track subleading components in certain parts of our analysis, as they are necessary to preserve the full momentum conservation.

In the
{\cal L}_{n}
SCET Lagrangian, uc gluons only couple to
n
-collinear fields through their
n\cdot A_{uc}
component, and in the
{\cal L}_{s}
SCET Lagrangian they only couple to the soft fields through their
\bar{n}\cdot A_{uc}
component. These interactions are eikonal and can be decoupled from the
n
-collinear and soft Lagrangians by suitable BPS field redefinitions
[
49
]
,

\displaystyle\chi_{n}\to{U}_{n}\chi_{n}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{n\perp}^{A}\to{\cal U}_{n}^{AB}\,{\cal B}_{n\perp}^{B}\,,{}

\displaystyle\psi_{s}^{n}\to{U}_{\bar{n}}\psi_{s}^{n}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{s\perp}^{nA}\to{\cal U}_{\bar{n}}^{AB}\,{\cal B}_{s\perp}^{nB}\,,

(3.45)

where the Wilson lines are defined by

\displaystyle{U}_{n}(x)=\bar{P}\exp\Big(-ig\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!ds\>n\cdot A^{A}_{uc}(ns+x)T^{A}\Big)\,,{}

\displaystyle{\cal U}_{n}(x)=\bar{P}\exp\Big(-ig\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!ds\>n\cdot A^{A}_{uc}(ns+x){\cal T}^{A}\Big)\,.

(3.46)

Here,
T^{A}
and
{\cal T}^{A}
are color generators in the fundamental and adjoint representations, respectively, and
\bar{P}
denotes anti-path-ordering of the color matrices.
Simply switching
n\to\bar{n}
gives the analogous definitions for
{U}_{\bar{n}}
and
{\cal U}_{\bar{n}}
. This field redefinition removes the uc interactions from
{\cal L}_{n}
and
{\cal L}_{s}
, but introduces uc interactions into the Glauber Lagrangian
{\cal L}_{G}^{(0)}
, including for the Glauber operators
{\cal O}_{ns}^{ij}
in eq. (
3.12
) relevant for the gap radiation. Using the color representation identities for Wilson lines

\displaystyle{U}_{n}^{\dagger}T^{B}{U}_{n}={\cal U}_{n}^{BB^{\prime}}T^{B^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle(-if^{BCD}){\cal U}_{n}^{CC^{\prime}}{\cal U}_{n}^{DD^{\prime}}={\cal U}_{n}^{BB^{\prime}}(-if^{B^{\prime}C^{\prime}D^{\prime}})\,,

(3.47)

we find that the Glauber operators
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{qB}
and
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{gB}
both have a single
{\cal U}_{n}^{DB}
, while the Glauber operators
\mathcal{O}_{s}^{q_{n}C}
and
\mathcal{O}_{s}^{g_{n}C}
both have a single
{\cal U}_{\bar{n}}^{DC}
.
Thus, the final result from the uc field redefinitions is independent of whether we have quark or gluon operators, and is simply

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{ns}

\displaystyle={\cal U}_{n}^{DB}{\cal U}_{\bar{n}}^{DC}\Big(\mathcal{O}_{n}^{B}\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{2}}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{C}\Big)\,.

(3.48)

This gives the modification to the
ns
Glauber operators in eq. (
3.16
) from the coupling of uc modes.
These uc modes contribute radiation to both the
X
and
Y
systems.

4
Factorization of Diffraction and Quasi-Diffraction

Now, we carry out a factorization of diffraction and quasi-diffraction. The similar underlying nature of these processes allows us to factorize both at the same time, using the exact same procedure. In
section
3.2
and
section
3.3
, we proved the necessary and sufficient constraints on our power counting parameters
\{\lambda,\,\lambda_{t},\,\rho,\,\lambda_{g},\,\lambda_{\Lambda}\}
in eq. (
3.17
) for a process to be (quasi-)diffractive. We start in
section
4.1
by factorizing (quasi-)diffraction in the case with the minimal assumption needed for a (quasi-)diffractive process; namely,
\lambda\ll 1
with no scaling assumptions on the other parameters in eq. (
3.17
); i.e. taking
\{\lambda_{t},\,\rho,\,\lambda_{g},\,\lambda_{\Lambda}\}\sim 1
.
In
section
4.2
, we discuss the additional hard-collinear factorization that arises if we take
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
,
and use this to present a Regge factorization formula for diffracitve PDFs.
We do not consider here the additional factorization that can arise from taking
\lambda_{t}\gg 1
and
\lambda\lambda_{t}\ll 1
.
We will consider some implications of the
\lambda_{\Lambda}\ll 1
expansion in
section
6
.

4.1
Factorization for
\lambda\ll 1

Our primary objective is to factorize the (quasi-)diffractive structure functions for an unpolarized initial proton (
i=2,L,3,4
).
Our factorization straightforwardly generalizes to the spin-polarized case, as we will see later in this section.
From eqs. (
2.2
) and (
2.45
), the full, unexpanded definition of these structure functions in QCD is

\displaystyle F_{i}^{D}(x,Q^{2},

\displaystyle\beta,t,m_{Y}^{2})=\mathcal{P}_{i\,\mu\nu}\sumint_{X,Y}\,\delta^{4}(q\!+\!p\!-\!p^{\prime}\!-\!p_{X})\,\big\langle p\big|J^{\mu}(0)\big|XY\big\rangle\big\langle XY\big|J^{\nu}(0)\big|p\big\rangle\delta(m_{Y}^{2}-p^{\prime\,2}){}

\displaystyle=x\,\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\,\mu\nu}\sumint_{X,Y}\,\int\!\!{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d}z\,e^{iz\cdot q}\>\big\langle p\big|J^{\mu}(z)\big|XY\big\rangle\big\langle XY\big|J^{\nu}(0)\big|p\big\rangle\delta(m_{Y}^{2}-p^{\prime\,2})\,,

where
{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d}z=d^{d}z/(2\pi)^{d}

and we switch to
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\,\mu\nu}
from eq. (
2.47
).
We use dimensional regularization with
d=4-2\epsilon
to handle any ultraviolet or infrared divergences. Note that we study the bare cross section first, before switching to renormalized quantities.

Momentum regions and operators for the
\lambda
expansion.

Let us consider the power counting parameters defined in eq. (
3.17
), setting
\lambda\ll 1
and keeping
\{\lambda_{t},\rho,\beta\}\sim 1
. Then, the Breit frame scaling results in eqs. (
3.26
) and (
3.28
) simplify to

\displaystyle q\sim\sqrt{s}(\lambda,\,\lambda,\,0)\,,

\displaystyle p_{X_{s}}\sim\sqrt{s}(\lambda,\,\lambda,\,\lambda)\,,

\displaystyle\tau\sim\sqrt{s}(\lambda^{3},\lambda,\lambda)

\displaystyle p\sim\sqrt{s}(0,\lambda^{-1},0)\,,

\displaystyle p^{\prime}_{n}\sim\sqrt{s}(\lambda^{3},\lambda^{-1},\lambda)\,.

(4.17)

As noted in
section
3.2.1
,
q
and
p_{X_{s}}
carry soft scaling (
s
),
\tau
is Glauber (
G
), and
p
and
p^{\prime}_{n}
are
n
-collinear.
We also must include radiation in
X
and
Y
that is soft enough to pass the rapidity gap restrictions, which from eq. (
3.32
) carries ultrasoft-collinear (uc)
momentum

\displaystyle p_{uc}^{\mu}\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda^{2}\>(\lambda,\lambda^{-1},1)\sim\sqrt{s}(\lambda^{3},\lambda,\lambda^{2})\,.

(4.18)

Let us analyze eq. (
4.1
) at leading power in SCET, using the scaling of particles in eq. (
4.1
). The hadronic current
J^{\mu}
attaches to a soft quark as in
figure
2
a, and hence in SCET can be replaced by a purely soft current,

\displaystyle J^{\mu}(z)\to J_{s}^{\mu}(z)=\sum_{q}e_{q}\bar{\psi}_{s}^{(q)}(z)\gamma^{\mu}\psi_{s}^{(q)}(z)\,,

(4.19)

where
q
indicates quark flavor. To study the hadronic states, we use an interaction picture where the factorizable components of the SCET Lagrangian,
{\cal L}_{n}^{(0)}+{\cal L}_{s}^{(0)}+{\cal L}_{uc}^{(0)}
, evolve the quantum states; meanwhile, we write out the non-factorizable component
{\cal L}_{G}^{(0)}
as insertions with the hadronic currents.
States in SCET can be organized such that they consist of particles from a single sector of the factorizable Lagrangian, or are direct product states from multiple sectors.
In (quasi-)diffraction,
|p\rangle
is entirely
n
-collinear at leading power. In contrast,
Y
can contain both
n
-collinear and uc particles, and
X
can contain both soft and uc particles. We factorize
|XY\rangle
into product states as

\displaystyle|XY\rangle=|X_{s}\rangle\>|Y_{n}\rangle\>|Z_{uc}\rangle\,,

(4.20)

where
X_{s}\subset X
contains soft particles,
Y_{n}\subset Y
describes
n
-collinear particles, and
Z_{uc}
involves uc particles coming from both
X
and
Y
; see
figure
6
.

To study the effects of Glauber exchange in diffraction, it is convenient to write the relevant terms of the Glauber action in eq. (
3.48
) as

\displaystyle\int d^{d}y\,{\cal L}_{G}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\int\![d\tilde{y}]\int\!\!\frac{{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d^{\prime}}\!\tau_{\perp}^{\prime}}{\tau_{\perp}^{\prime\,2}}\>{\cal O}_{n}^{B}(\tilde{y},\tau_{\perp}^{\prime})\>{\cal O}_{s}^{C}(\tilde{y},-\tau_{\perp}^{\prime})\>\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{BC}(\tilde{y})\,.

(4.21)

Here, we abbreviate
\tilde{y}^{\mu}=n\cdot y\bar{n}^{\mu}/2+\bar{n}\cdot yn^{\mu}/2
and
[d\tilde{y}]=\frac{1}{2}dy^{+}dy^{-}
, and we take the transverse integral over
d^{\prime}=d-2
dimensions. We Fourier-transform the Glauber operators into transverse momentum space as
{\cal O}_{n}^{A}(y^{\pm},\tau_{\perp}^{\prime})=\int d^{d^{\prime}}\!y_{\perp}\exp(-iy_{\perp}\cdot\tau_{\perp}^{\prime}){\cal O}_{n}^{A}(y)
and similarly for
{\cal O}_{s}^{A}(y^{\pm},-\tau_{\perp}^{\prime})
. We also introduce a shorthand for the product of adjoint Wilson lines:

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{BC}(\tilde{y})\equiv{\cal U}_{n}^{DB}(\tilde{y})\,{\cal U}_{\bar{n}}^{DC}(\tilde{y})\,.

(4.22)

The internal fields in
{\cal O}_{n}^{B}
and
{\cal O}_{s}^{C}
exactly conserve the
p_{n}^{-}\sim\lambda^{0}
and
p_{s}^{+}\sim\lambda
momenta, so the locality of these operators at
y^{\pm}
in eq. (
4.21
) encodes momentum conservation for
\tau^{-}\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda
and
\tau^{+}\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda^{3}
. Note that
\tau^{-}
is a leading momentum for the soft operator, but subleading to the
n
-collinear operator, as
\tau^{-}\ll p^{\prime\,-}
. Likewise,
\tau^{+}
is a leading momentum for the
n
-collinear operator, but subleading for the soft operator, as
\tau^{+}\ll p_{X}^{+},q^{+}
. For now, we allow soft and
n
-collinear operators to fully conserve momentum in subleading momentum components, but we will soon carry out multipole expansions of these operators.

Factorization.

A breadth of past literature on SCET informs our factorization procedure for diffraction.
Our ability to carry out a systematic factorization is enabled by ref.
[
51
]
, which points out in
\lx@sectionsign 8
that order by order in insertions of the Glauber Lagrangian, we can factorize soft and collinear interactions to all orders in
\alpha_{s}
.
A useful benchmark for our factorization comes from the Glauber SCET factorization of small-
x
DIS in ref.
[
189
]
, which studied squared matrix elements of Glauber operators involving a single exchange on each side of the cut, to sum logs of
x
at NLL order.
(Compared to this small-
x
DIS analysis, our diffractive case involves the additional
\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}
, and hence is in a
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
type theory. Additionally, for simplicity we treat the transverse coordinates in momentum space from the start.)
To inform the all-orders renormalization of our factorization, we use elements from the forward
2\to 2
scattering amplitudes analysis in ref.
[
87
]
, which carried out an all-orders factorization and renormalization using Glauber Lagrangians.
We also note certain parallels in techniques between our work and ref.
[
197
]
, which carries out an all-orders factorization for DIS off a heavy nucleus in the limit where Glaubers interact with color-uncorrelated partons in different nucleons, in order to show how saturation arises in an top-down EFT framework. Our factorization analysis in this section treats the Glauber Lagrangian to all orders for diffractive scattering off a DIS proton target, with only a small-
x
expansion.

To start, we combine the structure functions in eq. (
4.1
), currents in eq. (
4.19
), states in eq. (
4.20
), and Glaubers in eq. (
4.21
) with techniques from ref.
[
51
]
to get:

\displaystyle F_{i}^{D}=x\!\!\!\!\sumint_{X_{s},Y_{n},Z_{uc}}\sum_{N,N^{\prime}=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-\mathrm{i})^{N}}{N!}\frac{(+\mathrm{i})^{N^{\prime}}}{N^{\prime}!}\int\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d^{\prime}}\!\!\tau_{i\perp}}{\tau_{i\perp}^{2}}[d\tilde{y}_{i}]\>\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\frac{{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d^{\prime}}\!\!\tau^{\prime}_{j\perp}}{\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime\,2}}[d\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{j}]\>\delta\big(m_{Y}^{2}-(p_{n}^{\prime}+p_{uc}^{Y})^{2}\big){}

\displaystyle\times\big\langle p\big|\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A_{1}}(\tilde{y}_{1},\tau_{1\perp})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A_{N}}(\tilde{y}_{N},\tau_{N\!\perp})\big|Y_{n}\big\rangle\big\langle Y_{n}\big|\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A^{\prime}_{1}}(\tilde{y}_{1}^{\prime},\tau_{1\perp}^{\prime})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}}(\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}},\tau_{N^{\prime}\!\perp}^{\prime})\big|p\big\rangle{}

\displaystyle\times\big\langle 0\big|\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A_{1}B_{1}}(\tilde{y}_{1})\cdots\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A_{N}B_{N}}(\tilde{y}_{N})\,\big|Z_{uc}\big\rangle\big\langle Z_{uc}\big|\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A_{1}^{\prime}B_{1}^{\prime}}(\tilde{y}_{1}^{\prime})\cdots\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}B^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}}(\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}})\big|0\big\rangle

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\,\mu\nu}\int\!\!{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d}z\,e^{iz\cdot q}\,\big\langle 0\big|\bar{T}\,J_{s}^{\mu}(z)\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{1}}(\tilde{y}_{1},-\tau_{1\perp})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{N}}(\tilde{y}_{N},-\tau_{N\!\perp})\big|X_{s}\big\rangle{}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\times\big\langle X_{s}\big|T\,J_{s}^{\nu}(0)\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{1}^{\prime}}(\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{1},-\tau_{1\perp}^{\prime})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}}(\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}},-\tau_{N^{\prime}\!\perp}^{\prime})\big|0\big\rangle\,.

(4.31)

Here
T
and
\bar{T}
are time and anti-time ordering operators, respectively,

p_{n}^{\prime}
is the momentum of the state
Y_{n}
, and
p_{uc}^{Y}
is the momentum of the ultracollinear radiation that ends up in the hadronic object
Y
.
Recall that to formulate the differential diffractive cross section we extracted a phase space integral for the momentum
p^{\prime}
of
Y
, which in this factorized form should be viewed as extracted from the
Y_{n}
phase space integrations.
In this form, the
n
-collinear matrix elements (2nd line), uc matrix elements (3rd line), and soft matrix elements (4th and 5th lines) have been factorized, albeit with infinite sums over the number of Glauber exchanges to the left and right of the cut (
N
and
N^{\prime}
respectively).
Note that we must have at least one Glauber exchange on each side of the cut to get a non-zero result, so
N,N^{\prime}\geq 1
. This is clear from the particles produced in the central region which have
p_{X}^{\pm}>0
, which is allowed due to
q^{+}>0
and
\tau^{-}>0
.

To derive the final factorization formula we carry out a number of steps to manipulate eq. (
4.1
), which we discuss in order:

1.

First, we use translation invariance to factor out transverse-momentum-conserving
\delta
-functions from the collinear matrix elements. This imposes
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tau_{i\perp}=-\tau_{\perp}
and

\sum_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\tau^{\prime}_{j\perp}=\tau_{\perp}
(where
\tau_{\perp}\equiv p_{\perp}-p^{\prime}_{\perp}
), and leaves a free integration over the transverse momentum of one operator in each matrix element. We capture this by introducing the notation

\displaystyle\bar{\cal O}_{n}^{A}(\tilde{y})\equiv\!\int\!{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d^{\prime}}\!\!\tau^{\prime\prime}_{\perp}\,{\cal O}_{n}^{A}(\tilde{y},\tau^{\prime\prime}_{\perp})\,.

(4.32)

2.

Second we exploit the
collapse
of Glauber interactions, which causes a significant simplification of graphs involving multiple Glaubers. Contracting soft and collinear fields that generate propagators between Glauber vertices and carrying out the rapidity-regulated
\pm
integrals for Glauber loops causes instantaneity in the
Glauber vertices’
\pm
light-cone coordinates
[
51
]
,
setting
y_{i}^{\pm}=y_{1}^{\pm}
and
y_{i}^{\prime\pm}=y_{1}^{\prime\pm}
.
This also sets the coordinate dependences in the uc matrix element to these values.
While the combinatorics of the propagator contractions eliminate a
1/(N!N^{\prime}!)
factor, it is regenerated by the collapsed integrals.
Although a Glauber rapidity regulator
\eta^{\prime}
is important at intermediate steps and for generating the correct
1/N!
prefactors, the collapsed answer does not have leftover dependence on the regulator. (A different rapidity regulator
\eta
is still needed for the soft and collinear matrix elements; see Ref.
[
179
]
.)
When neighboring Glaubers are attached to the same soft and collinear lines in a graph, the collapse eliminates the intermediate propagators between them. When Glaubers are attached to different lines in a graph there is still a removal of propagators, but the collapse modifies the leftover external propagators such that they now carry a sum of various Glauber light-cone and transverse energies. See Ref.
[
51
]
for examples.

After these first two steps we have

\displaystyle F_{i}^{D}=x\!\!\!\sumint_{X_{s},Y_{n},Z_{uc}}\sum_{N,N^{\prime}=1}^{\infty}\int\!\!\!\!\!\int^{\perp}_{(N,\,N^{\prime})}\int[d\tilde{y}_{1}]\>[d\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{1}]\>\delta\big(m_{Y}^{2}-(p_{n}^{\prime}+p_{uc}^{Y})^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\times\big\langle p\big|\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}$}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A_{i}}(\tilde{y}_{1},\tau_{i\perp})\bar{}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A_{N}}(\tilde{y}_{1})\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|Y_{n}\big\rangle\big\langle Y_{n}\big|\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}-1}$}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A^{\prime}_{j}}(\tilde{y}_{1}^{\prime},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime})\bar{}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}}(\tilde{y}_{1}^{\prime})\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|p\big\rangle{}

\displaystyle\times\big\langle 0\big|\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A_{1}B_{1}}(\tilde{y}_{1})\cdots\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A_{N}B_{N}}(\tilde{y}_{1})\,\big|Z_{uc}\big\rangle\big\langle Z_{uc}\big|\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A_{1}^{\prime}B_{1}^{\prime}}(\tilde{y}_{1}^{\prime})\cdots\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}B^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}}(\tilde{y}^{\prime}_{1})\big|0\big\rangle

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\,\mu\nu}\int\!\!{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d}z\,e^{iz\cdot q}\,\big\langle 0\big|\bar{T}\,J_{s}^{\mu}(z)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{i=1}^{N}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{i}}\!(\tilde{y}_{1},-\tau_{i\perp})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|X_{s}\big\rangle\big\langle X_{s}\big|T\,J_{s}^{\nu}(0)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{j}^{\prime}}\!(\tilde{y}_{1}^{\prime},-\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|0\big\rangle\,.{}

Here, we follow ref.
[
87
]
and use a compact notation for the transverse momentum integrals

\displaystyle\int\!\!\!\!\!\int^{\perp}_{(N,\,N^{\prime})}\!\!\equiv\frac{(-\mathrm{i})^{N}(\mathrm{i})^{N^{\prime}}}{N!\,N^{\prime}!}\!\int\!\prod_{i=1}^{N}\!\frac{{d\!\!{}^{-}}\!^{d^{\prime}}\!\tau_{i\perp}}{\vec{\tau}_{i\perp}^{\,2}}\,{\delta\!\!\!{}^{-}}^{d^{\prime}}\!\Bigl(\sum_{i}\tau_{i\perp}+\tau_{\perp}\Bigr)\int\!\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}\frac{{d\!\!{}^{-}}\!^{d^{\prime}}\!\tau^{\prime}_{j\perp}}{{\vec{\tau}_{j\perp}^{\,\prime 2}}}\,{\delta\!\!\!{}^{-}}^{d^{\prime}}\!\Bigl(\sum_{j}{\tau}^{\prime}_{j\perp}-\tau_{\perp}\Bigr)\,,

(4.41)

where
{\delta\!\!\!{}^{-}}^{d^{\prime}}(\tau_{\perp})=(2\pi)^{d^{\prime}}\delta^{d^{\prime}}(\tau_{\perp})
.
Note that to the order we work in the
\lambda\ll 1
expansion,
\tau_{\perp}^{2}=-\vec{\tau}_{\perp}^{2}=t
.
The brackets
\{\!\!\{...\}\!\!\}
in eq. (
4.1
) indicate that the operators inside have undergone collapse, and hence appear at the same
\pm
lightcone coordinates. Note that these operators can still contract independently with different fields, such as the two quark fields in the currents
J^{\mu}
and
J^{\nu}
.
We provide explicit calculations involving these collapsed operator products in
section
6
.
For example, if
N^{\prime}=2
and we consider a generic contraction in the proton matrix element, then there are four
different contractions in the final soft matrix element. The collapse brackets are normalized such that for
N=1
,

\displaystyle\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\bar{}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A}(\tilde{y}_{1})\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}

\displaystyle=\bar{}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A}(\tilde{y}_{1})\,,

\displaystyle\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B}\!(\tilde{y}_{1},-\tau_{\perp}^{\prime})\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B}\!(\tilde{y}_{1},-\tau_{\perp}^{\prime})\,.

(4.42)

The remaining steps involve carrying out appropriate multipole expansions for the soft and collinear matrix elements:

3.

As a third step, we remove the
y_{1}^{-}
and
y_{1}^{\prime\,-}
dependences of the
n
-collinear and uc operators by carrying out a multipole expansion of the soft function. To see this, we first factor these dependences out of the collinear and uc matrix elements using translation invariance. This leads to a phase
\exp[\frac{i}{2}(y_{1}^{-}-y_{1}^{\prime-})(p^{+}-p_{n}^{\prime+}-p_{uc}^{+})]
, which feeds momenta into the soft matrix element. However, we can drop this phase because its momentum is much smaller than the intrinsic soft particle momenta encoded by soft fields depending on
y_{1}^{-}
and
y_{1}^{\prime\,-}
; namely,
(p^{+}-p_{n}^{\prime+}-p_{uc}^{+})\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda^{3}\ll p_{X_{s}}^{+}\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda
.

4.

Fourth, we factor out the
y_{1}^{+}
and
y_{1}^{\prime\,+}
dependences of the
n
-collinear and uc operators, which provides a nontrivial injection of momenta into the soft function.
Recall that the external kinematics fix the forward scattering condition
p^{\prime-}_{n}\simeq p^{-}\sim\lambda^{-1}
, whereas for the residual momentum
p^{-}-p_{n}^{\prime-}=\tau^{-}+p_{uc}^{Y-}\sim\lambda
. Using translation invariance, we factor out this small residual momentum from the collinear matrix elements and the momentum
p_{uc}^{-}
from the uc matrix element, giving the phase
\exp[-\frac{i}{2}(y_{1}^{\prime+}-y_{1}^{+})(\tau^{-}+p_{uc}^{Y-}-p_{uc}^{-})]=\exp[-\frac{i}{2}(y_{1}^{\prime+}-y_{1}^{+})(\tau^{-}-p_{uc}^{X-})]
. To leading order in
\lambda
, we can set
\tau^{-}=Q/\beta
in the Breit frame, but we leave it as
\tau^{-}
to emphasize the boost invariance of our final results. Since
\tau^{-}-p_{uc}^{X-}\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda
is the same size as
p_{X_{s}}^{-}\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda
, this phase injects this momentum into the soft matrix element. We rewrite this phase with two extra integration variables as

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{i}{2}(y_{1}^{\prime+}-y_{1}^{+})(\tau^{-}-p_{uc}^{X-})}=\int\!\!dk_{s}^{-}dp_{g}^{-}\>\delta(\tau^{-}-p_{g}^{-}-k_{s}^{-})\>\delta(p_{g}^{-}-p_{uc}^{X-})\>e^{-\frac{i}{2}(y_{1}^{\prime+}-y_{1}^{+})k_{s}^{-}}\,,

(4.43)

in order to factor pieces of the expression together with the uc and soft matrix elements.

5.

Fifth,
we manipulate the
\delta
-function on the first line of eq. (
4.1
). Using eq. (
3.44
), we can write its argument to leading power as

\displaystyle m_{Y}^{2}-(p-\tau)^{2}\simeq m_{Y}^{2}-t+p^{-}\tau^{+}=p^{-}(p^{\prime+}+p^{+}-p_{n}^{\prime+}-p_{uc}^{Y+})\,.

(4.44)

This enables us to write the
\delta
-function as a suitable phase

\displaystyle\delta(m_{Y}^{2}-(p_{n}^{\prime}+p_{uc}^{Y})^{2})

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{1}{2p^{-}}\int dv^{-}\>e^{\frac{i}{2}v^{-}(p^{\prime+}+p^{+}-p^{\prime+}_{n}-p_{uc}^{Y+})}

(4.45)

\displaystyle=\!\int\!\!dk_{n}^{+}dp_{g}^{+}\>\delta(p^{\prime+}-k_{n}^{+}-p_{g}^{+})\>\delta(p_{g}^{+}-p_{uc}^{Y+})\!\int\!\!\frac{dv^{-}}{2p^{-}}\>e^{\frac{i}{2}v^{-}(k_{n}^{+}+p^{+}-p_{n}^{\prime+})}.{}

We could set
p^{\prime+}=Qz
in the Breit frame, but we choose to leave it as
p^{\prime+}
to emphasize the boost invariance of our final results. In the second line, we have introduced two extra integration variables to factor pieces of the expression together with the uc and
n
-collinear matrix elements.
We then use
\exp(iv^{-}(p^{+}-p_{n}^{\prime+})/2)
to shift the (collapsed) collinear operators in a matrix element to the coordinate
v^{-}
.

Finally, we let
\tilde{y}_{1}=\tilde{y}
and
\tilde{y}_{1}^{\prime}=\tilde{y}^{\prime}
. This gives

\displaystyle F_{i}^{D}=x\!\!\!\sum_{N,N^{\prime}=1}^{\infty}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int^{\perp}_{(N,\,N^{\prime})}\int\!\!dk_{n}^{+}dk_{s}^{-}dp_{g}^{+}dp_{g}^{-}\>\delta(p^{\prime+}-k_{n}^{+}-p_{g}^{+})\>\delta(\tau^{-}-k_{s}^{-}-p_{g}^{-}){}

\displaystyle\times\!\!\int\!\!\frac{dv^{-}}{2p^{-}}e^{\frac{i}{2}v^{-}k_{n}^{+}}\sumint_{Y_{n}}\big\langle p\big|\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}$}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A_{i}}(v^{-},\tau_{i\perp})\bar{}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A_{N}}(v^{-})\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|Y_{n}\big\rangle\big\langle Y_{n}\big|\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}-1}$}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A^{\prime}_{j}}(0,\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime})\bar{}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}}(0)\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|p\big\rangle{}

\displaystyle\times\sumint_{Z_{uc}}\delta\bigl(p_{g}^{+}-p_{uc}^{Y+}\bigr)\,\delta\bigl(p_{g}^{-}-p_{uc}^{X-}\bigr)\,\big\langle 0\big|\bar{T}\,\,\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{i=1}^{N}$}\,\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A_{i}B_{i}}(0)\,\big|Z_{uc}\big\rangle\big\langle Z_{uc}\big|T\,\,\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}$}\,\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A^{\prime}_{j}B^{\prime}_{j}}(0)\,\big|0\big\rangle{}

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\,\mu\nu}\int\!\!{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d}\!z\>[d\tilde{y}]\,[d\tilde{y}^{\prime}]\>e^{iz\cdot q}\,e^{\frac{i}{2}(y^{+}-y^{\prime+})k_{s}^{-}}\,\sumint_{X_{s}}\big\langle 0\big|\bar{T}\,J_{s}^{\mu}(z)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{i=1}^{N}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{i}}\!(\tilde{y},-\tau_{i\perp})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|X_{s}\big\rangle{}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\big\langle X_{s}\big|T\,J_{s}^{\nu}(0)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{j}^{\prime}}\!(\tilde{y}^{\prime},-\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|0\big\rangle\>\,,

(4.70)

where the second line involves purely
n
-collinear matrix elements, the third line involves purely uc matrix elements, and the last two lines involve purely soft matrix elements. We depict the spacetime and momentum flows for this expression in
figure
9
.

Figure 9
:

Spacetime coordinates and momenta in the factorization formula for diffractive structure functions.
For simplicity, we only show Glaubers attaching to a single line in
S
and
B
and do not show any radiation in
U
. Note that our convention is such that both
\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}
and
\tau_{i\perp}
are incoming momenta for the soft function (on both sides of the cut).

The
p_{g}^{+}
and
p_{g}^{-}
momenta in the uc matrix element correspond to components in the Breit frame regions
Y
and
X
respectively, as shown in
figure
7
b. Below, we find it convenient to relate these to hemisphere regions, separated by the dashed boundary shown in
figure
7
b. The boost that takes the
X
-
Y
boundary to the hemisphere partition is given by
p_{H}^{+}=x^{-1/2}e^{{\bar{\eta}_{0}}}p^{+}
and
p_{H}^{-}=x^{1/2}e^{-{\bar{\eta}_{0}}}p^{-}
.
We can implement this by writing

\displaystyle x^{1/2}e^{-{\bar{\eta}_{0}}}\delta\bigl(p_{g}^{+}-p_{uc}^{Y+}\bigr)\,x^{-1/2}e^{{\bar{\eta}_{0}}}\delta\bigl(p_{g}^{-}-p_{uc}^{X-}\bigr){}

\displaystyle\quad=\delta\bigl(x^{-1/2}e^{{\bar{\eta}_{0}}}p_{g}^{+}-p_{uc}^{\,{\cal H}_{Y}+}\bigr)\,\delta\bigl(x^{1/2}e^{-{\bar{\eta}_{0}}}p_{g}^{-}-p_{uc}^{\,{\cal H}_{X}-}\bigr){}

\displaystyle\quad=\delta\Bigl(\frac{1}{h\sqrt{x}}\,p_{g}^{+}-p_{uc}^{\,{\cal H}_{Y}+}\Bigr)\,\delta\Bigl(h\sqrt{x}\,p_{g}^{-}-p_{uc}^{\,{\cal H}_{X}-}\Bigr)\,,

(4.71)

where
p_{uc}^{{\cal H}_{Y}+}
and
p_{uc}^{{\cal H}_{X}-}
are momentum components in hemisphere regions,

\displaystyle p_{uc}^{{\cal H}_{Y}+}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}p_{i\,H}^{+}\,\theta\bigl(p_{i\,H}^{-}-p_{i\,H}^{+}\bigr)\,,

\displaystyle p_{uc}^{{\cal H}_{X}-}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}p_{i\,H}^{-}\,\theta\bigl(p_{i\,H}^{+}-p_{i\,H}^{-}\bigr)\,.

(4.72)

To agree more closely with how experimental measurements distinguish
X
and
Y
, here we fix
{\bar{\eta}_{0}}
using eq. (
3.37
), and write the answer in terms of
e^{-{\bar{\eta}_{0}}}=h=\gamma e^{-\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}}/\sqrt{y}
from eq. (
3.38
), with
\gamma^{2}=E_{p}/E_{e}
the ratio of proton and electron energies in the lab frame.

Next, we decompose the octet color indices by introducing sums over irreducible color representations (irreps), again following the notation of ref.
[
87
]
.
Letting
\alpha=A_{1}\cdots A_{m}
be the indices for
m
gluons, the decomposition into irreps involves a sum over projectors

P_{m\,R}^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}
, which project an
m
-gluon color state onto a subspace
R
, and whose construction can be found in
[
198
]
. These projectors satisfy completeness and orthogonality relations

\displaystyle\delta_{m}^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{R}P_{m\,R}^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}=\sum_{R}P_{m\,R}^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime\prime}}P_{m\,R}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle P_{m\,R}^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\,P_{m\,R^{\prime}}^{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}=\delta_{RR^{\prime}}P_{m\,R}^{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{3}}.

(4.73)

In eq. (
4.1
), we utilize two copies of
\delta_{N}^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}
and two copies of
\delta_{N^{\prime}}^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}
to project the
\{A_{i}\}
,
\{B_{i}\}
,
\{A^{\prime}_{i}\}
, and
\{B^{\prime}_{i}\}
color indices. For example, the matrix elements on the RHS of the cut give

\displaystyle({\cal O}_{n}\cdots{\cal O}_{n})^{\alpha}({\cal U}\cdots{\cal U})^{\alpha\beta}({\cal O}_{s}\cdots{\cal O}_{s})^{\beta}{}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{R,R^{\prime}}({\cal O}_{n}\cdots{\cal O}_{n}P_{NR})^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}(P_{NR}\,{\cal U}\cdots{\cal U}P_{NR^{\prime}})^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\beta^{\prime\prime}}(P_{NR^{\prime}}{\cal O}_{s}\cdots{\cal O}_{s})^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}\,.

(4.74)

Note that the vacuum state
|0\rangle
, proton state
|p\rangle
, and currents
J^{\mu}
are all color singlets, but the states
|Y_{n}\rangle
and
|X_{s}\rangle
inherit the color representation of the operator in their corresponding matrix elements. Since these same states appear once in each of the
n
-collinear and soft matrix elements, the color representations
R
and
R^{\prime}
must have the same dimension on both sides of the cut. This implies
R_{A}=R_{A^{\prime}}
and
R_{B}=R_{B^{\prime}}
. However, these representations can be formed from a different number of gluon indices, so we can still have
N\neq N^{\prime}
. We therefore denote the common color representations by
R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}
and
R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}
.

Figure 10
:

Factorization of diffractive and quasi-diffractive
ep
scattering for
\lambda\ll 1
. Here, we see the convolution of a soft function describing the central jet, a beam function describing the hadronic system, and an ultrasoft-collinear (UC) function describing radiation penetrating the gap. Red dashed lines indicate Glauber (
G
) modes.

Using these results, we further tidy up eq. (
4.1
) by defining bare beam, soft, and uc functions containing the associated factorized matrix elements

\displaystyle F_{i}^{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{Q^{2}\varsigma^{2}}{x}\!\!\sum_{N,N^{\prime}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\{R_{X}\}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int^{\perp}_{(N,N^{\prime})}\int\!\!dk_{n}^{+}dk_{s}^{-}dp_{g}^{+}dp_{g}^{-}\>\delta\bigl(p^{\prime+}-k_{n}^{+}-p_{g}^{+}\bigr)\,\delta\bigl(\tau^{-}-k_{s}^{-}-p_{g}^{-}\bigr){}

\displaystyle\quad\ \ \times B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\bigl(k_{n}^{+}p^{-},\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},t\bigr)\ U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{h\sqrt{x}}\,p_{g}^{+},h\sqrt{x}\,p_{g}^{-},\mu\Bigr){}

\displaystyle\quad\ \ \times S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\bigl(k_{s}^{-}q^{+},\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},Q,t\bigr)\,,

(4.75)

Here,
\varsigma=x\,(\bar{n}\cdot p)(n\cdot q)/Q^{2}
enables us to define
B
and
S
in a manner that is boost invariant with respect to the
n
-
\bar{n}
axis; in the Breit frame,
\varsigma=1
.
More succinctly, carrying out the
k_{s}^{-}
and
k_{n}^{+}
integrals,

\displaystyle F_{i}^{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{Q^{2}\varsigma^{2}}{x}\!\!\sum_{N,N^{\prime}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\{R_{X}\}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int^{\perp}_{(N,N^{\prime})}\int\!\!dp_{g}^{+}dp_{g}^{-}\ B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\Bigl(p^{-}(p^{\prime+}-p_{g}^{+}),\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},t\Bigr){}

\displaystyle\quad\times U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{h\sqrt{x}}\,p_{g}^{+},h\sqrt{x}\,p_{g}^{-},\mu\Bigr)\ S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\Bigl(q^{+}(\tau^{-}-p_{g}^{-}),\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},Q,t\Bigr).

(4.76)

For later convenience, we define the shorthand notation

\displaystyle F_{i}^{D}\,\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{N,N^{\prime}}\sum_{R}B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\otimes_{\perp}S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\otimes_{\pm}U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}{}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{N,N^{\prime}}\sum_{R}(-i)^{N}(+i)^{N^{\prime}}B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\tilde{\otimes}_{\perp}S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\otimes_{\pm}U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\,.

(4.77)

In the second line we explicitly factored out
(-i)^{N}(+i)^{N^{\prime}}
from the
\otimes_{\perp}
perpendicular-momentum convolution, which will be used in a discussion below.
The soft function
S
is a matrix element describing the production of the central jet from the electromagnetic current and soft Glauber operators.
S
is the only term in the factorization that depends on the choice of structure function
i
. The beam function
B
describes the hadronic system: it is either a matrix element representing the transition of a proton into a multi-particle state through Glauber exchanges (incoherent), or a proton that remains intact in the presence of the Glauber exchanges (coherent).
The gap function
U
describes ultrasoft-collinear radiation that enters the rapidity gap and passes any imposed experimental restrictions.
These functions are tied together by transverse momenta from the Glauber exchanges and by the fact that while collinear radiation only contributes to
Y
and soft radiation only contributes to
X
, the uc radiation can contribute to both.

The collinear beam function in eq. (
4.1
) projected onto the color representation
R_{A}
is

\displaystyle B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\bigl(p^{-}k_{n}^{+},\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},t\bigr)=\frac{1}{(\bar{n}\cdot p)^{2}}\int\!\frac{dv^{-}}{2p^{-}}\>e^{\frac{i}{2}v^{-}k_{n}^{+}}\sumint_{Y_{n}}

\displaystyle\ \times\big\langle p\big|\!\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}$}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A_{i}}(v^{-}\text{\small$\frac{n}{2}$},\tau_{i\perp})\bar{}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A_{N}}(v^{-}\text{\small$\frac{n}{2}$})\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}P_{NR_{A}}\big|Y_{n}\big\rangle\big\langle Y_{n}\big|P_{N^{\prime}R_{A}}\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}-1}$}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A^{\prime}_{j}}(0,\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime})\bar{}\mathcal{O}_{n}^{A^{\prime}_{N^{\prime}}}(0)\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|p\big\rangle.{}

Here the
1/(\bar{n}\cdot p)
factors make
B
boost invariant along the
n
-
\bar{n}
axis.
We use this boost invariance and power counting to determine the form of the first argument of
B
.
Due to the presence of the state
Y_{n}
,
B
also depends implicitly on
h
, despite the fact that it is not shown as one of its arguments.
Note that while this beam function encodes the fundamental hadronic dynamics, eq. (
4.1
) is not identical to any known PDF or related object like transverse momentum distributions (TMDs)
[
199
]
or generalized parton distributions (GPDs)
[
200
,
201
,
202
]
.
12
12

12

Since our
B
is differential in
t
, it gives more direct information about the dynamics of the initial-state hadron and struck parton than the analogous beam function in small-
x
inclusive DIS
[
189
]
.
In particular, even for the color singlet
R_{A}^{NN^{\prime}}=1
, there are still an infinite number of functions indexed by
N,N^{\prime}\geq 2
.
The soft function with projection onto color representation
R_{B}
is

\displaystyle S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\bigl(q^{+}k_{s}^{-},\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},Q,t\bigr)=\Big(\frac{Q}{n\cdot q}\Big)^{2}\,\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\,\mu\nu}\!\!\int\![d\tilde{y}]\,[d\tilde{y}^{\prime}]\,{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d}\!z\ e^{\frac{i}{2}(y^{+}-y^{\prime+})k_{s}^{-}}e^{iz\cdot q}\,

(4.86)

\displaystyle\ \>\times\sumint_{X_{s}}\big\langle 0\big|\bar{T}\,J_{s}^{\mu}(z)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{i=1}^{N}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{i}}\!(\tilde{y},-\tau_{i\perp})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}P_{NR_{B}}\big|X_{s}\big\rangle\big\langle X_{s}\big|P_{N^{\prime}R_{B^{\prime}}}T\,J_{s}^{\nu}(0)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{j}^{\prime}}\!(\tilde{y}^{\prime},-\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|0\big\rangle.{}

Here the factors of
Q/n\cdot q
make
S
boost invariant along the
n
-
\bar{n}
axis, and
Q/n\cdot q=1
in the Breit frame.
We use boost invariance and power counting to determine the first argument of
S
.
The presence of the state
X_{s}
implies that
S
depends implicitly on
h
.
Finally, we have the ultrasoft-collinear function, which allows a transition between color representations
R_{A}
and
R_{B}
, and uses hemispheres to define momenta
\bar{p}_{uc}^{X-}
and
\bar{p}_{uc}^{Y+}
in the regions
X
and
Y
:

\displaystyle U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\!\!\bigl(p_{H}^{+},p_{H}^{-}\bigr)=\sumint_{Z_{uc}}\delta\Bigl(p_{H}^{+}-p_{uc}^{{\cal H}_{Y}+}\Bigr)\,\delta\Bigl(p_{H}^{-}-p_{uc}^{{\cal H}_{X}-}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\times\big\langle 0\big|P_{NR_{A}}\bar{T}\,\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{i=1}^{N}$}\,\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A_{i}B_{i}}(0)\,P_{NR_{B}}\big|Z_{uc}\big\rangle\big\langle Z_{uc}\big|P_{N^{\prime}R_{A^{\prime}}}T\,\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}$}\,\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{A^{\prime}_{j}B^{\prime}_{j}}(0)P_{N^{\prime}R_{B^{\prime}}}\big|0\big\rangle.{}

We recall from eq. (
4.22
) that
\mathbb{U}_{n\bar{n}}^{AB}
is the product of adjoint Wilson lines.
In the arguments of eqs. (
4.1
) through (
4.1
), we suppress dependence on regulators, though all three functions depend on
\epsilon
, and
B
and
S
depend on an additional rapidity regulator
\eta
.

In
section
2
, we used Lorentz invariance to show that
F_{i}^{D}
is not a function of
y
, which seems to be contradicted by the the
y
-dependence inside
h
in eq. (
4.1
). This is not a contradiction because we spoil Lorentz invariance when defining
X
and
Y
with a rapidity cut in the lab frame, and this breaking is encoded by the appearance of
h
.
If we instead imposed a fixed rapidity cut
{\bar{\eta}_{0}}
in the
\gamma_{\bar{n}}
-frame, we would have
h=e^{-{\bar{\eta}_{0}}}
and thus would retain
y
-independence of
F_{i}^{D}
.
This is also true if we impose a fixed rapidity cut in the Breit frame with a constant
h_{0}
, see eq. (
3.42
), where the factorization formula simplifies through the replacement
h\sqrt{x}\to h_{0}
.
For a lab frame rapidity cut
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
it is convenient to write

\displaystyle h\sqrt{x}=\frac{x\gamma\sqrt{s}}{Q}e^{-\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}}\,,

(4.103)

which makes the dependence on collider variables explicit.

Setting
d^{\prime}=2
in eq. (
4.1
), we see that
F_{i}^{D}
has mass dimension
-4
, which equals the dimension of
\int\!\!\!\!\!\int^{\perp}_{(N,\,N^{\prime})}
in eq. (
4.41
).
The function
S
is dimensionless;
B
has dimension
-2
, which compensates for the dimension of the prefactor; whereas
U
has mass dimension
-2
, which compensates for the
+2
dimension from
dp_{g}^{+}\,dp_{g}^{-}
.

From eq. (
2.2
), we
recall that the diffractive structure functions
F_{i}^{D}

must be real, and we can reorganize eq. (
4.1
) to make this apparent in the factorized expression.
Taking complex conjugates, we find that
B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{*}=B_{(N^{\prime},N)}
,
S_{i(N^{\prime},N)}^{*}=S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}
, and
U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{*}=U_{(N^{\prime},N)}
.
These equalities must be understood with the color indices and arguments associated with matrix elements to left and right of the cut appropriately swapped. For example,
\Bigl(B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\bigl(p^{-}k_{n}^{+},\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},t\bigr)\Bigr)^{*}=B_{(N^{\prime},N)}^{R_{A}^{N^{\prime}\!N}}\bigl(p^{-}k_{n}^{+},\{\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime},\tau_{i\perp}\},t\bigr)
, where the transverse momenta on the right-side of the cut of
B_{(N,N^{\prime})}
are identified with those of the left-side of the cut of
B_{(N^{\prime},N)}
.
We can now group different terms in eq. (
4.1
) as

\displaystyle F_{i}^{D}=

\displaystyle\sum_{N}\sum_{R}(-i)^{N}(+i)^{N}B_{(N,N)}^{R_{A}^{N\!N}}\tilde{\otimes}_{\perp}S_{i(N,N)}^{R_{B}^{N\!N}}\otimes_{\pm}U_{(N,N)}^{R_{A}^{N\!N}R_{B}^{N\!N}}{}

\displaystyle+\sum_{N<N^{\prime}}\sum_{R}\Bigg[(-i)^{N}(+i)^{N^{\prime}}B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\tilde{\otimes}_{\perp}S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\otimes_{\pm}U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}{}

\displaystyle\hskip 54.06006pt+(-i)^{N^{\prime}}(+i)^{N}B_{(N^{\prime},N)}^{R_{A}^{N^{\prime}\!N}}\tilde{\otimes}_{\perp}S_{i(N^{\prime},N)}^{R_{B}^{N^{\prime}\!N}}\otimes_{\pm}U_{(N^{\prime},N)}^{R_{A}^{N^{\prime}\!N}R_{B}^{N^{\prime}\!N}}\Bigg]\,,

(4.104)

where each term is explicitly real by complex conjugation properties, manifestly demonstrating that
F_{i}^{D}
is real, as expected.

Renormalization.

Next, we consider the impact of renormalization on our bare factorization in eq. (
4.1
). Using ref.
[
87
]
and knowledge of renormalization in the di-hemisphere mass distribution factorization
[
203
]
, we find that
B
,
S
, and
U
are renormalized multiplicatively in their momentum function space (i.e., with convolutions over all momentum arguments that appear as integrals in eq. (
4.1
)), and diagonal in the reduced color representations
R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}
and
R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}
.
B
and
S
exhibit standard ultraviolet renormalization in the
\overline{\rm MS}
scheme that leaves dependence on the invariant mass renormalization scale
\mu
, as well as rapidity renormalization that leaves dependence on a (mass-dimension-1) rapidity scale
\nu
. To treat rapidity renormalization, we follow refs.
[
204
,
205
]
.
U
has no rapidity divergences, so the renormalized
U
is only
\mu
dependent. Renormalization group (RG) consistency in SCET implies that the rapidity anomalous dimensions of
B
and
S
are equal and opposite, while the
\mu
anomalous dimensions of
B
,
S
, and
U
sum to zero when combined with suitable integrations. These considerations lead to a renormalized factorization formula,

\displaystyle F_{i}^{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{Q^{2}\varsigma^{2}}{x}\!\!\sum_{{\footnotesize N,N^{\prime}=1}}^{\infty}\!\sum_{\{R_{X}\}}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int^{\perp}_{(N,N^{\prime})}\int\!\!dp_{g}^{+}dp_{g}^{-}\ B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\Bigl(m_{Y}^{2}-t-p^{-}p_{g}^{+},\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},t,\mu,\frac{\nu}{Q/x}\Bigr){}

\displaystyle\!\!\times\!U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\!\!\!\Big(\frac{1}{h\sqrt{x}}\,p_{g}^{+},h\sqrt{x}\,p_{g}^{-},\mu\Big)S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\!\Bigl(\frac{Q^{2}}{\beta}\!-\!q^{+}p_{g}^{-},\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},Q,t,\mu,\frac{\nu}{Q/\beta}\Bigr).

(4.105)

Here, we substitute Breit frame variables with Lorentz invariants:
p^{-}p^{\prime+}=m_{Y}^{2}-t
and
q^{+}\tau^{-}=Q^{2}/\beta
.
The beam function gains dependence on the proton momentum
p^{-}=Q/x
due to boost invariance breaking by the rapidity regulator in the argument
\frac{\nu}{Q/x}
.

Diffraction and quasi-diffraction.

Experimental cross sections include contributions from both diffraction (color-singlet
R=1
) and quasi-diffraction (all
R\neq 1
channels), as
F_{i}^{D}=F_{i}^{D\,{\rm diff}}+F_{i}^{D\,{\rm quasi}}
.
Eq. (
4.1
) allows us to clarify the distinction between these two contributions.

Let us begin by discussing diffraction, which proceeds via a color-singlet channel. This occurs for all
N,N^{\prime}\geq 2
, and can occur with multiple copies for larger
N
and
N^{\prime}
. The factorization in eq. (
4.1
) simplifies significantly for diffraction: the uc function becomes trivial for color-singlet exchange, irrespective of the value of
N
and
N^{\prime}
, to all orders in
\alpha_{s}
:

\displaystyle U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{1\,1}\bigl(\bar{p}_{g}^{+},\bar{p}_{g}^{-}\bigr)=\delta(\bar{p}_{g}^{+})\delta(\bar{p}_{g}^{-})\,.

(4.106)

Simply put, at leading power there is automatically no ultrasoft-collinear radiation into the gap for diffraction. This occurs because the color-singlet projection cancels out all Wilson lines
{\cal U}_{n}
and separately all Wilson lines
{\cal U}_{\bar{n}}
.
For example, for
N=2
, we have

\displaystyle P_{2\,1}{\cal U}_{n}^{C_{1}A_{1}}{\cal U}_{\bar{n}}^{C_{1}B_{1}}{\cal U}_{n}^{C_{2}A_{2}}{\cal U}_{\bar{n}}^{C_{2}B_{2}}

\displaystyle\propto\delta^{A_{1}A_{2}}{\cal U}_{n}^{C_{1}A_{1}}{\cal U}_{n}^{C_{2}A_{2}}{\cal U}_{\bar{n}}^{C_{1}B_{1}}{\cal U}_{\bar{n}}^{C_{2}B_{2}}=\delta^{B_{1}B_{2}}\,.

(4.107)

Similar algebra eliminates the Wilson lines for any
(N,N^{\prime})
for singlet exchange.
Thus, for diffraction we have a much simpler renormalized factorization formula

\displaystyle F_{i}^{D\,{\rm diff}}\!

\displaystyle=\frac{Q^{2}\varsigma^{2}}{x}\!\!\sum_{{\footnotesize N,N^{\prime}=1}}^{\infty}\sum_{R^{N\!N^{\prime}}=1}\int\!\!\!\!\!\int^{\perp}_{(N,N^{\prime})}\!\!B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\Bigl(m_{Y}^{2}-t,\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},t,\frac{\nu}{Q/x}\Bigr){}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\Bigl(\frac{Q^{2}}{\beta},\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},Q,t,\frac{\nu}{Q/\beta}\Bigr).

(4.108)

Here,
B
and
S_{i}
are separately
\mu
-independent because there is no uc function; i.e., there is no RG evolution in
\mu
between them, as they exist at the same invariant mass scale in our analysis so far.
The dependence on parameters like
h
drops out and only enters through the definition of the
X
and
Y
states.

Quasi-diffraction, in contrast, proceeds via color-nonsinglet channels, which occur for all
N,N^{\prime}\geq 1
. In this case, the uc function is nontrivial, so quasi-diffraction is described by the full renormalized factorization formula in eq. (
4.1
) minus the singlet channel:

\displaystyle F_{i}^{D\,{\rm quasi}}\!=\frac{Q^{2}\varsigma^{2}}{x}\!\!\!\!\sum_{{\footnotesize N,N^{\prime}=1}}^{\infty}\sum_{R_{X}\neq 1}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int^{\perp}_{(N,N^{\prime})}\int\!\!dp_{g}^{+}dp_{g}^{-}\,B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\!\Bigl(m_{Y}^{2}-t\!-\!p^{-}p_{g}^{+},\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},t,\mu,\frac{\nu}{Q/x}\Bigr){}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \times U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\!\Bigl(\frac{1}{h\sqrt{x}}\,p_{g}^{+},h\sqrt{x}\,p_{g}^{-},\mu\Bigr)\>S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\!\Bigl(\frac{Q^{2}}{\beta}\!-\!q^{+}p_{g}^{-},\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},Q,t,\mu,\frac{\nu}{Q/\beta}\Bigr).

(4.109)

Thus, the quasi-diffractive process gives a non-trivial contribution to the total diffractive cross section, and even with cuts, is a nonzero irreducible background to the color singlet diffractive process.
Note that
h
is the only explicit parameter that scales with the power counting
\lambda_{g}
from eq. (
3.17
).
We discuss the magnitude of quasi-diffraction relative to diffraction in
section
7
.

We emphasize that the results in eqs. (
4.1
) and (
4.1
) apply for the experimental
m_{X}
-method and coherent forward detector method discussed in sections
2.3
and
3.3
. For the rapidity gap method, an additional scale
E_{\rm gap}^{\rm lab}
enters due to the constraint
E_{i}^{\rm lab}<E_{\rm gap}^{\rm lab}
for
\eta_{\rm min}^{\rm lab}<\eta_{i}^{\rm lab}<\eta_{\rm max}^{\rm lab}
, which modifies the allowed radiation in
X
. Therefore,
E_{\rm gap}^{\rm lab}
,
\eta_{\rm min}^{\rm lab}
, and
\eta_{\rm max}^{\rm lab}
would appear as additional arguments for the functions
S
and
U
.
Although it is tractable to analyze this method in our setup, we do not do so here.

The diffractive factorization in eq. (
4.1
) includes contributions from all singlet Glauber gluon exchanges, including different singlet channels (Pomeron, odderon, etc.), as well as both perturbative and non-perturbative contributions.
When
\sqrt{-t}\gg\Lambda_{\rm QCD}
, we say that a perturbative (hard) Pomeron contribution occurs when
\tau_{i\perp}\sim\sqrt{-t}\gg\Lambda_{\rm QCD}
for all
i
, which we can calculate with an expansion in
\alpha_{s}(\sqrt{-t})
. A non-perturbative Pomeron contribution occurs when
\tau_{i\perp}\sim\Lambda_{\rm QCD}
for one or more
i
, in which case there is no coupling constant suppression from adding the corresponding Glauber exchange operator. We integrate over the
\tau_{i\perp}
variables, so both of these contribute for
\sqrt{-t}\gg\Lambda_{\rm QCD}
. For the situation where
\sqrt{-t}\sim\Lambda_{\rm QCD}
, all Glauber gluons are nonperturbative with
\tau_{i\perp}\sim\Lambda_{\rm QCD}
, corresponding to the so-called soft Pomeron. Quasi-diffraction likewise has perturbative and non-perturbative contributions for various color channels. We explore this separation of perturbative and nonperturbative contributions further in
section
6
.

So far we have only carried out the factorization that arises from the
\lambda\ll 1
expansion. In
section
4.2
, we consider an additional expansion in
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
that further factorizes
S_{i}
. We leave exploration of the re-factorization of
B
for
\lambda_{\Lambda}=\Lambda_{\rm QCD}/\sqrt{-t}\ll 1
to a future publication.

Spin-dependent structure functions.

From our
\lambda\ll 1
factorization, we can now understand why the six polarized structure functions listed in eq. (
2.55
) contribute to (quasi-)diffraction at leading power, but the remaining eight in
appendix
A.1.1
do not. Due to factorization, the proton spin
S
only enters the beam function. The beam function is a scalar in Lorentz space because the Glauber exchanges have a fixed structure and do not transmit spin information. Thus, we can decompose
B
into two terms:

\displaystyle\widehat{B}_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R}=B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R}+\tilde{S}_{T}\>B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{TR}\,,

(4.110)

In the Breit frame,
\tilde{S}_{T}=\epsilon_{\perp}^{\alpha\beta}\tau^{\perp}_{\alpha}S^{T}_{\beta}/\sqrt{-t}
with
\epsilon_{\perp}^{\alpha\beta}\equiv\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}\bar{n}_{\gamma}n_{\delta}/2
, and hence is an allowed structure in
B
.
Here
B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R}
appears for the unpolarized structure functions
F_{L,2,3,4}^{D}
and the antisymmetric structure function
F_{4A}^{D}
, with the hadron beam unpolarized and the lepton beam polarized, and
B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{TR}
appears for hadron-spin-polarized structure functions
\propto\tilde{S}_{T}
.
Thus, only the structure functions
F_{iP}^{D}
with simple multiplicative hadronic spin dependence can be nontrivial at this order, and we predict that their leading-power factorization formulas are given by

\displaystyle F_{iP}^{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{X}}{\sqrt{-t}}\>F_{i}^{D}\ \text{with}\ B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R}\to B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{TR}\,,

(4.111)

with
i=L,2,3,4,4A
and
F_{i}^{D}
given by eqs. (
1.2
), (
4.1
), and (
4.1
).

In contrast, the structure functions in
appendix
A.1.1
with tensor coefficients
w_{i}^{\mu\nu}
for
i=5
-
8
or
i=5A
-
8A
are proportional to either
\bar{U}
or
\bar{X}
. In the Breit frame, we have

\displaystyle\bar{X}^{\mu}=\epsilon_{\perp}^{\mu\alpha}S^{T}_{\alpha}\,,\qquad\bar{U}^{\mu}=\frac{n^{\mu}+\bar{n}^{\mu}}{2}\>\tilde{S}_{T}^{\prime}-\epsilon_{\perp}^{\mu\alpha}\tau^{\perp}_{\alpha}\>\frac{S_{L}}{\gamma N_{X}}\,,

(4.112)

where
S_{L}
is the
z
-component of the spin vector
S^{\mu}
as defined in eq. (
A.13
).
These structure functions will include prefactors
\epsilon_{\perp}^{\mu\alpha}k^{\perp}_{\mu}S^{T}_{\alpha}
or
\epsilon_{\perp}^{\mu\alpha}k^{\perp}_{\mu}p^{\prime}_{\alpha}
, and are impossible to generate at leading power due to the scalar nature of
B
, which does not allow for a contraction of transverse Lorentz indices with the soft function
S_{i}^{\mu\nu}
.

4.2
Hard-collinear factorization of
S^{\mu\nu}
for
\lambda\ll 1
and
\lambda_{t}\ll 1

Next, we consider the case where we keep
\lambda\ll 1
and
{\rho,\beta}\sim 1
as in
section
4.1
, but now impose
\lambda_{t}=\sqrt{-t}/Q\ll 1
. This
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
expansion will induce a further factorization beyond eqs. (
4.1
) and (
4.1
).
We can deduce from the dependence on scales how this additional factorization will play out for (quasi-)diffraction. Examining eq. (
4.1
), we see that only the soft function
S
depends on both
t
and
Q^{2}
, which means that a
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
expansion can impact its structure. Since the beam function
B
only depends on
t
,
m_{Y}^{2}
and
\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}
, it will remain unaffected by a
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
expansion.

To make this more precise, let us examine how the momenta in eqs. (
3.26
), (
3.28
),
and (
3.32
) scale in
\lambda,\lambda_{t}
:

\displaystyle q\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda(1,\,1,\,0)\,,

\displaystyle p_{X_{s}}\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda(1,\,1,\,\lambda_{t})\,,

\displaystyle\tau\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda(\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2},1,\lambda_{t})\,,

\displaystyle p\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda(0,\lambda^{-2},0)\,,

\displaystyle p^{\prime}_{n}\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda(\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2},\lambda^{-2},\lambda_{t})\,,
