\displaystyle p_{uc}^{\mu}\sim\sqrt{s}\lambda\big(\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2},1,\lambda\lambda_{t}\big)\,.

(4.113)

The combination
\sqrt{s}\lambda\sim Q
appears as a prefactor in all the momenta and serves as an overall hard scale for the problem. The modes
\tau
and
q
have collinear and hard scalings in the parameter
\lambda_{t}
, respectively. This implies that the momentum
p_{X_{s}}
in the soft-
\lambda
region with
q+\tau=p_{X}=p_{X_{s}}+p_{uc}^{X}
has contributions from both types of modes. A
\lambda_{t}
-refactorization of the soft function entails separating loop and outgoing momenta with these hard-
\lambda_{t}
and collinear-
\lambda_{t}
scalings.

Our factorization procedure here closely parallels the hard-collinear factorization used in inclusive DIS. Specifically, we can
refactorize the soft function in eq. (
4.86
) into a hard function
\mathcal{H}
and a soft-collinear (sc) function
S_{\rm c}
. From the EFT perspective,
\mathcal{H}
encodes the hard-
\lambda_{t}
momentum region and serves as the Wilson coefficient for
S_{\rm c}
, which encodes the collinear-
\lambda_{t}
momenta. For this analysis it is convenient to write the first argument of the
S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R^{N\!N^{\prime}}}
soft function as
Q^{2}u/\beta
, where
u=1
for diffraction, and
u=1-p_{g}^{-}/\tau^{-}
for quasi-diffraction. The minus component of both modes scales as
\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{t}^{0})
, so they are coupled by a convolution integral:

\displaystyle S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R^{N\!N^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{Q^{2}u}{\beta},\{\tau_{k\perp},\tau_{\ell\perp}^{\prime}\},Q,t,\mu,\frac{\nu}{Q/\beta}\Bigr)

(4.114)

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{2}}{Q^{2}u^{2}}\sum_{\kappa}\int_{\beta}^{u}\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}\>\mathcal{H}^{\kappa}_{i}\Bigl(\frac{\beta}{\zeta},Q,\mu\Bigr)\>S^{\kappa;R^{N\!N^{\prime}}}_{\rm c(N,N^{\prime})}\left(\frac{\zeta}{u},\{\tau_{k\perp},\tau_{\ell\perp}^{\prime}\},t,\frac{\nu}{Q/\beta},\mu\right)\times\left[1+\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{t})\right]{}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\beta^{2}}{Q^{2}u^{2}}\,\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\kappa}\otimes_{-\>}S^{\kappa;R^{N\!N^{\prime}}}_{\rm c(N,N^{\prime})}\times\left[1+\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{t})\right]\,,{}

where
u>\beta
since
p_{X_{s}}^{-}>0
.
Here
{\cal H}_{i}^{\kappa}
captures the hard
\lambda_{t}
-regime and
S_{\rm c}
describes the collinear-
\lambda_{t}
regime, and encodes the color and functional dependence of
S^{\mu\nu}
that talks to
B
(and
U
).
Physically, it is easiest to understand the form of the convolution in eq. (
4.114
) in the Breit frame. The original
S_{i}
depends on two minus-momenta, through
Q^{2}u/\beta=q^{+}(\tau^{-}u)
and
Q^{2}=q^{+}(-q^{-})
. We can view eq. (
4.114
) as describing the
(N,N^{\prime})
Glaubers which enter the soft function with total minus-momentum
\tau^{-}u
, splitting into a quark or gluon
\kappa
that carries momentum
p_{\kappa}^{-}
, which then participates in the hard scattering. The
S_{\rm c}
function depends on the momentum fraction
p_{\kappa}^{-}/(u\tau^{-})=\zeta/u
, while the hard function

{\cal H}_{i}^{\kappa}
depends on the momentum fraction
(-q^{-})/p_{\kappa}^{-}=(-q^{-}/\tau^{-})(\tau^{-}/p_{\kappa}^{-})=\beta/\zeta
, and thus eq. (
4.114
) factorizes the dependence on the two original minus-momenta into individual functions.
Recall that
\beta=-q^{-}/\tau^{-}
in the Breit frame.
The last line in eq. (
4.114
) introduces a shorthand
\otimes_{-}
for the
\zeta
momentum fraction convolution.

Results for dPDFs.

In the 1990s, refs.
[
52
,
53
]
established a hard-collinear factorization framework for diffraction with
\lambda_{t}=\sqrt{-t}/Q\ll 1
, which resembles the factorization approach used in inclusive DIS and our hard-collinear factorization of
S
in eq. (
4.114
).
This factorization for coherent diffraction is written as
[
42
]

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x}F^{D}_{2/L}=\sum_{\kappa}\int_{\beta}^{1}\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}H^{\kappa}_{2/L}\Big(\frac{\beta}{\zeta},Q,\mu\Big)f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm coh}}\left(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},t,\mu\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{-t}{Q^{2}},\frac{\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right)\,.

(4.115)

Here,
H^{\kappa}_{2/L}
is the same hard coefficient as for inclusive DIS, and
f_{\kappa}^{D}
is the diffractive PDF (dPDF) for a parton of type
\kappa
. The
\mu
-dependence of
H^{\kappa}_{i}
and
f_{\kappa}^{D}
is governed by DGLAP evolution, which relates diffractive structure functions at different
Q
. The dPDF factorization formula in eq. (
4.115
) forms the basis of many HERA global fits
[
10
,
104
,
206
,
207
,
143
,
208
]
and is the subject of many model calculations and generalizations
[
129
,
209
,
210
,
211
,
212
]
.

The literature frequently notes the lack of a first-principles description of how the hadronic sector forward-scatters; i.e., the Regge factorization of
f_{i}^{D}

[
53
,
52
,
213
,
82
,
57
]
, which corresponds to our
\lambda\ll 1
expansion.
We can resolve this issue by combining our
\lambda\ll 1
factorization from
section
4.1
with the
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
factorization of the soft function in eq. (
4.114
). Note that beyond just the
F_{2,L}^{D}
often considered in diffraction, our results also apply to
F_{3,4}^{D}
and to quasi-diffractive processes.
Substituting eq. (
4.114
) into eq. (
4.1
) and eq. (
4.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x}\,F_{i}^{D\,{\rm diff}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\kappa}\int_{\beta}^{1}\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}\>\mathcal{H}^{\kappa}_{i}\Bigl(\frac{\beta}{\zeta},Q,\mu\Bigr)f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm diff}}\Bigl(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},m_{Y}^{2}-t,t,\mu\Bigr)\times\big[1+{\cal O}(\lambda_{t})\big]\,,{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x}\,F_{i}^{D\,{\rm quasi}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\kappa}\int_{\beta}^{1}\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}\>\mathcal{H}^{\kappa}_{i}\Bigl(\frac{\beta}{\zeta},Q,\mu\Bigr)f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm quasi}}\Bigl(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},m_{Y}^{2}-t,t,\mu\Bigr)\times\big[1+{\cal O}(\lambda_{t})\big]\,,

(4.116)

where using a shorthand
m^{2}=m_{Y}^{2}-t
, the diffractive PDF is

\displaystyle f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm diff}}\Bigl(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},m^{2},t,\mu\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\varsigma^{2}\!\!\sum_{{\footnotesize N,N^{\prime}=1}}^{\infty}\sum_{R^{N\!N^{\prime}}=1}\frac{\beta^{2}}{x^{2}}\int\!\!\!\!\!\int^{\perp}_{(N,N^{\prime})}\!\!S^{\kappa;R^{N\!N^{\prime}}}_{\rm c(N,N^{\prime})}\left(\zeta,\{\tau_{k\perp},\tau_{\ell\perp}^{\prime}\},t,\frac{\nu}{Q/\beta},\mu\right){}

\displaystyle\ \ \times B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\Bigl(m^{2},\{\tau_{k\perp},\tau_{\ell\perp}^{\prime}\},t,\frac{\nu}{Q/x}\Bigr)\times\big[1+{\cal O}(\lambda)\big],

(4.117)

and the quasi-diffractive PDF is

\displaystyle f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm quasi}}

\displaystyle\Bigl(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},m^{2},t,\mu\Bigr)=\varsigma^{2}\!\!\sum_{{\footnotesize N,N^{\prime}=1}}^{\infty}\sum_{R_{X}\neq 1}\!\int\!\!\!\!\!\int^{\perp}_{(N,N^{\prime})}\int\!\!dp_{g}^{+}dp_{g}^{-}\>U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\!\Bigl(\frac{p_{g}^{+}}{h\sqrt{x}},h\sqrt{x}\,p_{g}^{-},\mu\Bigr){}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\beta^{2}}{x^{2}}\left(\frac{\tau^{-}}{\tau^{-}-p_{g}^{-}}\right)^{2}\>S^{\kappa;R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}_{\rm c(N,N^{\prime})}\left(\zeta\frac{\tau^{-}}{\tau^{-}-p_{g}^{-}},\{\tau_{k\perp},\tau_{\ell\perp}^{\prime}\},t,\frac{\nu}{Q/\beta},\mu\right)\,\theta\big(\tau^{-}(1-\zeta)-p_{g}^{-}\big){}

\displaystyle\times B_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}^{N\!N^{\prime}}}\!\Bigl(m^{2}-p^{-}p_{g}^{+},\{\tau_{k\perp},\tau_{\ell\perp}^{\prime}\},t,\mu,\frac{\nu}{Q/x}\Bigr)\times\big[1+{\cal O}(\lambda)\big]\,.

(4.118)

Note that the nontrivial
x/\beta
dependence in the (quasi-)diffractive PDF is only induced by the sum of large rapidity logarithms between the soft-collinear and beam functions.
The total dPDF is given by the sum of these two contributions

\displaystyle f_{\kappa}^{D}\Bigl(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},m^{2},t,\mu\Bigr)

\displaystyle=f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm diff}}\Bigl(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},m^{2},t,\mu\Bigr)+f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm quasi}}\Bigl(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},m^{2},t,\mu\Bigr)\,.

(4.119)

These diffractive results can also be decomposed into coherent and incoherent contributions, see
section
7.4
and
section
7.5
.
This result marks the first explicit Regge factorization of diffractive PDFs and of the new quasi-diffractive PDFs in eq. (
4.2
).
Note that the analysis above is based on the consistency of results obtained separately in the
\lambda\ll 1
and
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
limits, rather than a more rigorous derivation based on an operator analysis, which we leave to future work.

Relationship to DIS.

Inclusive DIS factorizes as
F_{i}=x\int_{x}^{1}d\xi/\xi\,H_{i}^{\kappa}(x/\xi,Q,\mu)f_{\kappa}(\xi,\mu)
. In constrast, the hard coefficient in diffraction depends on
\beta=Q^{2}/2q\cdot\tau
instead of
x=Q^{2}/2q\cdot p
, since the momentum fraction
\zeta=p_{\kappa}^{-}/\tau^{-}
is relative to the incoming Glauber momentum
\tau^{-}=Q/\beta
rather than the incoming proton momentum
p^{-}=Q/x
. (Recall that we always expand in
\lambda\lambda_{t}\ll 1
, in which case
\tau^{-}/p^{-}=x/\beta
.) A further distinction between DIS and diffraction is that when
\lambda_{\Lambda}=\Lambda_{\rm QCD}/\sqrt{-t}\ll 1
, it is possible to perturbatively match
S_{\rm c}\otimes_{\perp}B
onto simpler nonperturbative matrix elements (though we do not do so here). Of course, if
\lambda_{\Lambda}\sim 1
,
S_{\rm c}
and
B
become fully nonperturbative just like PDFs.

Inclusive and diffractive DIS have identical tensor structures for
w_{2}^{\mu\nu}
and
w_{L}^{\mu\nu}
; see eq. (
2.2
). Thus, we expect that the hard coefficients
\mathcal{H}^{\kappa}_{2/L}
and
H^{\kappa}_{2/L}
of diffraction and DIS are identical functions,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{j}_{2/L}(\zeta,Q,\mu)=H_{2/L}^{j}(\zeta,Q,\mu)\,.

(4.120)

For
F_{2}
and
F_{L}
, these matching coefficients at NLO are
[
156
,
214
,
215
]
:

\displaystyle H^{q_{j}[0]}_{2}(\zeta,Q,\mu)

\displaystyle=e_{j}^{2}\delta(1-\zeta)\,,{}

\displaystyle H^{q_{j}[1]}_{L}(\zeta,Q,\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{F}e_{j}^{2}}{\pi}\zeta\,,{}

\displaystyle H^{q_{j}[1]}_{2}(\zeta,Q,\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{s}C_{F}e_{j}^{2}}{4\pi}\left[4\left(\frac{\ln(1-\zeta)}{1-\zeta}\right)_{+}-3\left(\frac{1}{1-\zeta}\right)_{+}-2(1+\zeta)\ln(1-\zeta)\right.{}

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-2\frac{1+\zeta^{2}}{1-z}\ln\zeta+6+4\zeta-\left(\frac{2\pi^{2}}{3}+9\right)\delta(1-\zeta)\right]\,,{}

\displaystyle H^{g[1]}_{2}(\zeta,Q,\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\frac{\alpha_{s}T_{F}e_{j}^{2}}{2\pi}\left[(1-2\zeta+2\zeta^{2})\ln\frac{Q^{2}(1-\zeta)}{\mu^{2}\zeta}-1+8\zeta-8\zeta^{2}\right]\,,{}

\displaystyle H^{g[1]}_{L}(\zeta,Q,\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\frac{\alpha_{s}T_{F}e_{j}^{2}}{2\pi}4\zeta(1-\zeta)\,,

(4.121)

where the superscript
[n]
indicates the result at
{\cal O}(\alpha_{s}^{n})
and
\sum_{j}
sums over quark and anti-quark flavors. For inclusive DIS,
H^{j}_{2/L}
is known at N
3
LO for both neutral and charged currents
[
216
,
217
,
218
]
. In
section
6.3
, we perturbatively compute
S_{\rm c}
for
N=N^{\prime}=1
and verify eq. (
4.120
). We leave the explicit operator definitions of
S_{\rm c}
to a future paper.

We can do a consistency check between the hard-collinear factorization of diffractive DIS in eq. (
4.115
) and inclusive DIS with our prefactors and the same hard coefficients. The two hadronic tensors are defined as

\displaystyle W_{D}^{\mu\nu}(q,p,p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sumint_{X}\delta^{4}(q+p-p^{\prime}-p_{X})\>\big\langle p\big|J^{\dagger\,\mu}(0)\big|p^{\prime}X\big\rangle\big\langle p^{\prime}X\big|J^{\nu}(0)\big|p\big\rangle\,,{}

\displaystyle W_{\rm DIS}^{\mu\nu}(q,p)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(2\pi)^{3}\sumint_{X}\>\delta^{4}(q+p-p_{X})\>\big\langle p\big|J^{\dagger\,\mu}(0)\big|X\big\rangle\big\langle X\big|J^{\nu}(0)\big|p\big\rangle\,,

where we restrict to coherent diffraction for convenience. In other words,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{d^{3}p^{\prime}}{2E_{p}^{\prime}}W_{D}^{\mu\nu}(q,p,p^{\prime})=W_{\rm DIS}^{\mu\nu}(q,p)\,.

(4.138)

The phase space of
p^{\prime}
includes a non-diffractive region. However, the hard-collinear factorization does not depend on this condition, and so the comparison is valid. Parameterizing
W_{D}^{\mu\nu}
and
W^{\mu\nu}
according to
section
2.2
, we find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{d^{3}p^{\prime}}{2E_{p^{\prime}}}F_{2/L}^{D}=\frac{\pi}{4}\int_{x}^{1}d\beta\int dt\,\frac{x}{\beta^{2}}F_{2/L}^{D}=F_{2/L}\,.

(4.139)

Hard-collinear factorization gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x}F_{2/L}^{D}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\kappa}\int_{\beta}^{1}\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}H^{\kappa}_{2/L}\Big(\frac{\beta}{\zeta},Q,\mu\Big)f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm coh}}\left(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},t,\mu\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{-t}{Q^{2}},\frac{\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right)\,,{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x}F_{2/L}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\kappa}\int_{x}^{1}\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}H^{\kappa}_{2/L}\Big(\frac{x}{\zeta},Q,\mu\Big)f_{\kappa}\left(\zeta,\mu\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}}{Q^{2}}\right)\,.

(4.140)

Therefore, eq. (
4.139
) implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}\int_{x}^{1}\!\!\!d\beta\,dt\,\frac{x}{\beta^{2}}\int_{\beta}^{1}\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}

\displaystyle H^{\kappa}_{2/L}\Big(\frac{\beta}{\zeta},Q,\mu\Big)f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm coh}}\Bigl(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},t,\mu\Bigr)=\int_{x}^{1}\!\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}H^{\kappa}_{2/L}\Big(\frac{x}{\zeta},Q,\mu\Big)f_{\kappa}\left(\zeta,\mu\right)\,.

(4.141)

Changing variables from
\beta
to
\zeta^{\prime}=x\zeta/\beta
, and then from
\zeta
to
\xi=x/\beta=\zeta^{\prime}/\zeta

gives

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}\int_{x}^{1}\!\!d\beta\int\!dt\frac{x}{\beta^{2}}\,

\displaystyle\int_{\beta}^{1}\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}\,H^{\kappa}_{2/L}\Big(\frac{\beta}{\zeta},Q,\mu\Big)\,f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm coh}}\left(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},t,\mu\right)

(4.142)

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{1}\!\frac{d\zeta^{\prime}}{\zeta^{\prime}}\,H^{\kappa}_{2/L}\Big(\frac{x}{\zeta^{\prime}},Q,\mu\Big)\left[\frac{\pi}{4}\zeta^{\prime}\int_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{1}\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta^{2}}\int dtf_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm coh}}\left(\zeta,\frac{x}{\beta},t,\mu\right)\right]{}

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{1}\frac{d\zeta^{\prime}}{\zeta^{\prime}}\,H^{\kappa}_{2/L}\Big(\frac{x}{\zeta^{\prime}},Q,\mu\Big)\left[\frac{\pi}{4}\int_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{1}d\xi\int dt\,f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm coh}}\left(\frac{\zeta^{\prime}}{\xi},\xi,t,\mu\right)\right]\,.{}

Using eq. (
4.141
) this implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}\int_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{1}d\xi\int dt\,f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm coh}}\left(\frac{\zeta^{\prime}}{\xi},\xi,t,\mu\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{d^{3}{p^{\prime}}}{2E^{\prime}_{p}}\,f_{\kappa}^{D\,{\rm coh}}\biggl(\frac{\zeta^{\prime}}{\xi}=\frac{{p}_{\kappa}^{-}/p^{-}}{\tau^{-}/p^{-}},\xi=\frac{\tau^{-}}{p^{-}},t=-\frac{\vec{p}_{\perp}^{\,{}^{\prime}2}}{1-\xi},\mu\biggr){}

\displaystyle=f_{\kappa}\left(\zeta^{\prime},\mu\right)\,.

(4.143)

This is consistent with the operator definitions

\displaystyle f_{q}\left(\zeta^{\prime},\mu\right)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{dy^{+}}{4\pi}\,e^{-i\zeta^{\prime}p^{-}y^{+}}\Bigl\langle p\Bigl|\bar{\psi}_{q}\Bigl(y^{+}\frac{\bar{n}}{2}\Bigr)\not{\bar{n}}\psi_{q}(0)\Bigr|p\Bigr\rangle\,,

(4.144)

\displaystyle f_{q}^{D\,{\rm coh}}\left(\frac{\zeta^{\prime}}{\xi},\xi,t,\mu\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sum_{X}\hskip-15.93347pt\large\int\normalsize\,\,\int\frac{dy^{+}}{4\pi}\,e^{-i\zeta^{\prime}p^{-}y^{+}}\Bigl\langle p\Bigl|\bar{\psi}_{q}\Bigl(y^{+}\frac{\bar{n}}{2}\Bigr)\not{\bar{n}}\Bigl|p^{\prime}X\Bigl\rangle\Bigr\langle p^{\prime}X\Bigr|\psi_{q}(0)\Bigr|p\Bigr\rangle\,,{}

for the quark PDF and dPDF, and similarly for the gluon case.

5
Large Logarithms in (Quasi-)Diffraction and Sudakov Suppression

As discussed in sections
2.3
and
3.2
, (quasi-)diffractive processes depend on a significant number of hierarchical momentum scales.

Figure
11
depicts these scales and their associated power counting expansion parameters.
Quantum fluctuations lead to large logarithms of ratios of these physical scales, which can spoil a strict perturbative expansion in
\alpha_{s}(\mu)\ll 1
even when
\mu\gg\Lambda_{\rm QCD}
. To obtain convergence, we can sum up the large logarithms to all orders in perturbation theory using RG techniques in the EFT.

We begin our discussion of the structure of large logarithms in the situation where
\lambda\ll 1
with
\lambda_{t}\sim 1
(whose factorization we discussed in
section
4.1
). We also analyze the additional large logarithms present when
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
(whose factorization we discussed in
section
4.2
).
Additionally, we address the special role played by the rapidity-cut-induced parameter
\lambda_{g}
in eq. (
3.17
).
Throughout this section, we treat
\lambda_{\Lambda}\sim 1
, and hence do not address additional logarithms that would appear from a hierarchy
\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}\ll-t
. We also do not consider the large logarithms that arise in the case of
\lambda_{t}\gg 1
with
\lambda\lambda_{t}\ll 1
.

Diffractive scales

Quasi-diffractive scales

Figure 11
:

Scale hierarchies in diffraction (left and middle graphics) and quasi-diffraction (middle and right graphics). The power counting parameters
\lambda_{i}
characterize ratios of scales indicated by the arrows (see also eq. (
3.17
)), and are shown for the case
\beta\sim 1
,
\rho\sim 1
,
\lambda_{t}\lesssim 1
and
\lambda\lambda_{t}\lesssim\lambda_{g}
. Here
\mu^{2}
indicates a separation in invariant mass, while
\nu
indicates a separation in longitudinal momentum, which tracks a separation in rapidity. Some of these hierarchies produce large logarithms, as discussed in the text.

5.1
Diffraction

For diffraction with color-singlet Glauber exchange, the
\lambda\ll 1
limit yields the factorization formula in eq. (
4.1
). In this factorization only rapidity logarithms appear, which correspond to
\ln(1/x)
terms, as shown in the center panel of
figure
11
. We can resum these logs using RG equations (RGEs) in the rapidity scale
\nu
, as rapidity evolution sums logarithms between the beam function scale
\nu_{n}=Q/x
and the soft function scale
\nu_{s}=Q/\beta
. At lowest order, this sums a single logarithmic series
\sim\sum_{k}a_{k}(\alpha_{s}\ln x)^{k}
. In the language of Pomeron exchange used in the diffraction literature
[
42
]
, this resummation corresponds to solving the BFKL equation for the Pomeron at the cross section level
[
219
]
.
For coherent diffraction, our beam function
B
in eq. (
4.1
)
is simply a squared amplitude (see
section
7.4
).
There is a large community studying the Regge limit of amplitudes, using modern field theory methods based on Reggeons
13
13

13

The original Reggeon field theory approach was formulated by Gribov and colleagues in Refs.
[
220
,
221
,
222
,
223
]
.

[
224
,
225
,
226
,
227
,
228
,
229
]
, the bootstrap approach
[
230
]
, and Glauber SCET
[
51
,
179
,
231
,
87
,
232
,
233
]
.
Interestingly, at the level of color-singlet two-Glauber exchange, our beam function involves the same operators as the amplitude-level collinear functions in Ref.
[
87
]
, for which the BFKL Pomeron RGE was derived.
By RG consistency, our diffractive soft function
S
(which involves a cross section-level sum over final states
X
) must be governed by the same Pomeron BFKL evolution. Furthermore, in multi-particle (incoherent) diffraction, this same
S
appears, and its rapidity logarithms must also be governed by the same amplitude-level Pomeron BFKL evolution (as must
B
for multi-particle diffraction).
We leave further numerical exploration of this resummation to future work.

Further power expansions in other power-counting parameters
\lambda_{i}\ll 1
or
\lambda_{i}\gg 1
induce new logarithms as well. In
section
4.2
, we examined how a
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
expansion factorizes the soft function into a hard function that depends on
Q^{2}
and
\mu
, and a soft-collinear function that depends on
t
and
\mu
. This diffractive hard function is related to the inclusive DIS hard function, and hence its RGE in
\mu
is governed by the DGLAP equations. The resummation associated to the
Q^{2}\gg-t
scale separation thus produces a single-logarithmic series for diffraction, which at leading order takes the form
\sim\sum_{k}b_{k}[\alpha_{s}\ln(-t/Q^{2})]^{k}
.
These invariant mass scales are pictured in the left graphic in
figure
11
.

The factorization formula for diffraction in eq. (
4.1
) does not depend on an ultrasoft collinear function
U
, and thus diffraction exhibits no logarithms coming from evolution between
U
and other scales. This applies for the small-
m_{X}
and coherent forward detector
methods used for diffractive measurements, as discussed in
section
3.3
.
In contrast, the “rapidity gap-method” induces sensitivity to an additional scale
E_{\rm gap}^{\rm lab}
in the soft function, as mentioned in
section
4.1
, but we do not treat the factorization for this case here. Depending on the relative hierarchies between
\sqrt{-t}
,
Q
, and
E_{\rm gap}^{\rm lab}
, this can induce further factorization of the soft function and require resummation of additional logarithms.

5.2
Quasi-diffraction

Like diffraction, the
\lambda\ll 1
factorization formula for quasi-diffraction in eq. (
4.1
) exhibits logarithms associated to the beam and soft rapidity scales
\nu_{c}
and
\nu_{s}
. The corresponding rapidity RGEs sum single-logarithmic series of
\alpha_{s}\ln x
terms, which correspond to BFKL-type equations for each contributing color channel at the cross section level. Quasi-diffraction also has
\alpha_{s}\ln(-t/Q^{2})
contributions in the
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
regime from DGLAP evolution, just like we discussed for diffraction in
section
5.1
.

However, unlike diffraction, the quasi-diffractive factorization in eq. (
4.1
) includes a non-trivial ultrasoft-collinear function
U
and thus
\mu
-evolution between
U
and
B
, as well as between
U
and
S_{i}
.
The presence of
U
induces sensitivity to invariant mass scales
\mu_{u1}
and
\mu_{u2}
that we can read off from the factorization formula in eq. (
4.1
):

\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{p_{g}^{+}}{h\sqrt{x}}\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\sim\mu_{u1}^{2}\simeq\frac{Q^{2}z^{2}}{xh^{2}}\sim\frac{\lambda^{4}\lambda_{t}^{4}}{\lambda_{g}^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\big(h\sqrt{x}p_{g}^{-}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle\sim\mu_{u2}^{2}\simeq\frac{xh^{2}Q^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\sim\lambda^{4}\lambda_{g}^{2}\,.

(5.1)

These two additional scales are shown in the right graphic in
figure
11
.
Note that due to the constraint
z\ll h^{2}
from eq. (
3.40
) that we always have
\mu_{u1}^{2}\ll\mu_{B}^{2}\sim-t\sim m_{Y}^{2}\sim Q^{2}z/x
.
Similarly,
xh^{2}\ll 1
from eq. (
3.41
) implies that
\mu_{u2}^{2}\ll\mu_{S}^{2}\sim Q^{2}\sim m_{X}^{2}\sim Q^{2}/\beta
. This condition corresponds to

\displaystyle x^{2}\ll\frac{\beta^{2}\mu_{S}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}s}\>e^{2\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}}\,,

(5.2)

which is always satisfied for large
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
, but starts to constrain the phase space for smaller
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
. For example, with
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}=3
and HERA kinematics, it corresponds to
x\ll 0.2
. The orange and green arrows in the right graphic of
figure
11
correspond to large double (Sudakov) logarithms that arise due to the
\mu_{u1}^{2}\ll\mu_{B}^{2}
and
\mu_{u2}^{2}\ll\mu_{S}^{2}
scale hierarchies.

The form of the quasi-diffractive factorization implies that the RG evolution of
U
in
\mu
involves contributions
related to both its
p_{g}^{+}/(h\sqrt{x})
and
h\sqrt{x}p_{g}^{-}
variables,
which factorize to all orders in perturbation theory. This directly generalizes results for the hemisphere soft function for the dijet invariant mass spectrum in refs.
[
234
,
203
]
and implies that

\displaystyle U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}R_{B}}(p_{H}^{+},p_{H}^{-},\mu)

\displaystyle=\!\!\int\!\!d\ell^{+}d\ell^{-}\,{\cal U}(p_{H}^{+}-\ell^{+},\mu,\mu_{0})\,{\cal U}(p_{H}^{-}-\ell^{-},\mu,\mu_{0})\,U_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}R_{B}}(\ell^{+},\ell^{-},\mu_{0}),{}

\displaystyle\tilde{U}_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}R_{B}}(y^{-},y^{+},\mu)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\cal U}(y^{-},\mu,\mu_{0})\,\tilde{\cal U}(y^{+},\mu,\mu_{0})\,\tilde{U}_{(N,N^{\prime})}^{R_{A}R_{B}}(y^{-},y^{+},\mu_{0})\,.

(5.3)

Here
{\cal U}
and its Fourier transform
\tilde{\cal U}
are evolution kernels that sum large logarithms (whose dependence on
(N,N^{\prime})
and
R_{A}R_{B}
we suppress for brevity). We see from the second line that in Fourier space, evolution is multiplicative.

It is convenient to factorize the boundary condition by utilizing different
\mu_{0}
values in the two evolution kernels
{\cal U}

[
234
]
, specifically,
\mu_{0}=\mu_{u1}
and
\mu_{0}=\mu_{u2}
. This helps us avoid unphysical
\ln(\mu_{u1}/\mu_{u2})
terms that can be induced by choosing a single
\mu_{0}
; see refs.
[
235
,
236
]
.
When combining the RG evolution of
U
with that of
B
and
S
, we also must run the two
{\cal U}
’s up to two different scales
\mu=\mu_{B}
and
\mu=\mu_{S}
, as appropriate for
B
and
S
. For the
\lambda\ll 1
expansion without an expansion in
\lambda_{t}
, these correspond to

\displaystyle\mu_{B}^{2}\simeq\{-t,m_{Y}^{2}\}\,,\qquad\qquad\mu_{S}^{2}\simeq\{Q^{2},Q^{2}/\beta,-t\}\,.

(5.4)

Taken together, the RG evolution in
\mu
sums large logarithms through the evolution factors
{\cal U}(\ell^{+},\mu_{B},\mu_{u1})
and
{\cal U}(\ell^{-},\mu_{S},\mu_{u2})
. The right panel of
figure
11
illustrates the ratio of scales associated to the summation of these logs using green and orange dashed arrows. The leading logarithms (LL) are an infinite series of Sudakov double logarithms, of the form
\sim\sum_{k}c_{k}[\alpha_{s}\ln^{2}(\mu_{B}/\mu_{u1})]^{k}
and
\sim\sum_{k}c_{k}[\alpha_{s}\ln^{2}(\mu_{S}/\mu_{u2})]^{k}
. The resummed result is a Sudakov form factor, and has the physical interpretation of suppressing quasi-diffraction due to a restriction forbidding radiation into a (gapped) region of phase space.

When we consider additional scale hierarchies beyond those induced by
\lambda\ll 1
, new large logarithms arise. Let us consider the
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
case discussed in
section
4.2
. Large logarithms appear in the soft function, which can be resummed by considering its refactorization into hard and soft-collinear functions. Just as we saw in diffraction in
section
5.1
, this involves a series of single logarithms at leading order
\sim\sum_{k}b_{k}^{\prime}[\alpha_{s}\ln(-t/Q^{2})]^{k}
, determined by DGLAP.
There are a number of allowed hierarchies involving the parameters
\lambda_{g}
and
\lambda_{t}
.
These can induce additional logarithms for
U
called non-global logarithms (NGLs)
[
237
]
, which start with a term
\sim\alpha_{s}^{2}\ln^{2}(\mu_{u1}/\mu_{u2})
. These are large logarithms unless
\lambda_{t}\sim\lambda_{g}
where
\mu_{u1}\sim\mu_{u2}
.
NGLs arise due to correlated emissions, where radiation into the phase space region
X
influences radiation that enters the region
Y
. The nonglobal structure of an analogue of our
U
(in a triplet rather than octet color channel) is known at
{\cal O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
from studies of the dihemisphere soft function
[
235
,
236
]
, with resummation at next-to-leading-logarithmic (NLL) order
[
237
,
238
,
239
,
240
,
241
,
242
]
.
Note that in contrast, diffraction does not exhibit NGLs due to the absence of
U
(and thus
\lambda_{g}
) as well as the control of
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
logs by DGLAP.

Below, we study in greater detail the Sudakov suppression of quasi-diffraction, which is one of the key features necessary to quantify the magnitude of color-nonsinglet contamination relative to the color-singlet signal in the diffractive cross section in
section
7
. At leading logarithmic (LL) order, we only need to calculate the Sudakov double logarithms from the ultrasoft collinear function and can neglect NGLs and BFKL logarithms, which only enter beyond LL order. To our knowledge, the precise size of this quasi-diffractive background has not been previously quantified in the literature.

5.2.1
One Glauber amplitudes in
q\bar{q}
scattering

We can extract a substantial amount of information about diffractive anomalous dimensions from existing results in the SCET literature.
First, let us build off a set of one-loop
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}

2\to 2
amplitude calculations from ref.
[
51
]
; in particular, of forward scattering an
n
-collinear quark and an
\bar{n}
-collinear antiquark in the presence of virtual soft and ultrasoft gluons and quarks.
For a single Glauber exchange, the
n
-collinear amplitude in ref.
[
51
]
is identical to the one appearing in our beam function
B
,
A^{n}=A^{n}_{\text{\tiny\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Rothstein:2016bsq}{}{}]}}}
, the ultrasoft amplitude is identical to our
U
,
A^{uc}=A^{us}_{\text{\tiny\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Rothstein:2016bsq}{}{}]}}}
, and remaining
\bar{n}
-collinear and soft amplitudes can be combined to provide a proxy for our soft amplitude,
A^{s}\simeq A^{\bar{n}}_{\text{\tiny\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Rothstein:2016bsq}{}{}]}}}+A^{s}_{\text{\tiny\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Rothstein:2016bsq}{}{}]}}}
. Although the combination of these forward scattering objects governs a different process than our desired soft amplitude (which produces a soft quark pair at tree level), the equivalence of the other amplitudes and RG consistency requires the structure of their logarithms to be related, and hence it provides a useful proxy for us to see how various scales can appear.

Using dimensional regularization
d=4-2\epsilon
, the
\eta
rapidity regulator, and offshellness IR regulators
p^{2}=m_{Y}^{2}
and
\bar{p}^{2}=m_{X}^{2}

[
51
]
(see §7.3), the amplitudes have logarithmic terms

\displaystyle A^{uc}

\displaystyle=\!A_{G}^{\rm tree}\frac{C_{A}\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{2\pi}\biggl(-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\ln\frac{\mu^{2}s}{m_{X}^{2}m_{Y}^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\ln^{2}\frac{\mu^{2}s}{m_{X}^{2}m_{Y}^{2}}\biggr)+\ldots\,,

(5.5)

\displaystyle A^{n}

\displaystyle=\!A_{G}^{\rm tree}\frac{C_{A}\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{2\pi}\biggl(\frac{g_{\epsilon}}{\eta}-\ln\frac{\nu}{p^{-}}\Bigl[\frac{1}{\epsilon}\!+\!\ln\frac{\mu^{2}}{-t}\Bigr]\!+\!\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\!+\!\frac{1}{\epsilon}\ln\frac{\mu^{2}}{m_{Y}^{2}}\!+\!\frac{1}{2}\ln^{2}\frac{\mu^{2}}{m_{Y}^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\ln^{2}\frac{m_{Y}^{2}}{-t}\biggr)+\ldots,{}

\displaystyle A^{s}

\displaystyle=\!A_{G}^{\rm tree}\frac{C_{A}\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{2\pi}\biggl(-\frac{g_{\epsilon}}{\eta}-\ln\frac{m_{X}^{2}}{\nu p_{s}^{+}}\Bigl[\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\ln\frac{\mu^{2}}{-t}\Bigr]+\ln^{2}\frac{-t}{m_{X}^{2}}+\frac{\beta_{0}}{2C_{A}}\ln\frac{\mu^{2}}{-t}\biggr)+\ldots\,,{}

where
A_{G}^{\rm tree}
stands for the tree-level amplitude, the coefficient of the
1/\eta
rapidity divergence is
g_{\epsilon}=e^{\epsilon\gamma_{E}}\cos(\pi\epsilon)\Gamma(1+\epsilon)\Gamma(-\epsilon)\big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{-t}\big)^{\epsilon}
, and
p_{s}^{+}\simeq s/p^{-}
.
The ellipses in eq. (
5.5
) denote non-logarithmic terms or terms with other color factors that are not needed for our examination of scales.
Each of these functions has its logarithms minimized at a common
(\mu,\nu)
scale choice, which we label as
(\mu_{i},\nu_{i})
with an index
i=\{S,U,B\}
corresponding to the choice of function.
In
A_{U}^{uc}
we would pick
\mu_{uc}^{2}\simeq m_{X}^{2}m_{Y}^{2}/s
, in
A_{B}^{n}
we would pick
\mu_{B}^{2}\simeq-t\sim m_{Y}^{2}
and
\nu_{B}\simeq p^{-}
, and in
A_{S}^{s}
we would pick
\mu_{s}^{2}\simeq m_{X}^{2}
and
\nu_{s}\simeq m_{X}^{2}/p_{X}^{+}\sim-t/p_{X}^{+}
.
The RG evolution in
\mu
and
\nu
allows us to choose these canonical scales
(\mu_{i},\nu_{i})
as the boundary conditions for the various functions, and then evolve them to a common
(\mu,\nu)
choice.

In our
\lambda
power counting, we have that
\mu_{uc}^{2}\sim\lambda^{4}
,
\mu_{B}^{2}\sim\lambda^{2}
,
\nu_{B}\sim\lambda^{-1}
,
\mu_{s}^{2}\sim\lambda^{2}
, and
\nu_{s}\sim\lambda
, which match with the assignments in
figure
11
. Evolving between these scales sums large logarithms.
When summing these amplitudes at one-loop, all explicit dependences on
\mu
and
\nu
cancel, except for the
\ln\mu
associated with the running coupling. We have

\displaystyle A^{us}+A^{n}+A^{s}=A_{G}^{\rm tree}\frac{C_{A}\alpha_{s}}{2\pi}\Big[-\frac{1}{2}\ln^{2}\frac{m_{X}^{2}m_{Y}^{2}}{-st}+\ln^{2}\frac{m_{X}^{2}}{-t}+\ln^{2}\frac{m_{Y}^{2}}{-t}+\frac{\beta_{0}}{2C_{A}}\ln\frac{\mu^{2}}{-t}\>\Big]\,,

(5.6)

giving Sudakov double logarithms.
Although we only present the pattern of logarithms for the virtual one-loop amplitudes from a single Glauber exchange here, the canonical scales apply equally well once we include additional logarithms from emission diagrams in the full beam, soft, and uc functions.
For the rapidity divergences
1/\eta
and their associated
\nu
-dependent logarithms, the results exactly match with the expected gluon Regge exponent
\propto[1/\epsilon+\ln\frac{\mu^{2}}{-t}]
multiplying the
\ln\nu
terms, and the real emission graphs simply generalize this to the full BFKL equation for the
8_{A}
color channel. (Note that the
1/\epsilon
here is UV due to ultrasoft 0-bin subtractions
[
51
]
.)

5.2.2
Sudakov suppression from
U
in quasi-diffraction

Due to RG consistency, we can carry out resummation at leading double-logarithmic order, using the
\mu
-RGE for the ultrasoft collinear function
U
. This enables us to predict the precise form of the LL Sudakov form factor, including which scales enter its double logarithms. In the region of perturbative invariant mass scales
Q^{2},-t,m_{X}^{2},m_{Y}^{2}\gg\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}
, color-octet single Glauber exchange (
N=N^{\prime}=1
) dominates
U
; hence, this is our focus in this section. Note that we expect other color channels to have similar LL Sudakov factors, differing simply in the leading color Casmir factor present in the Sudakov exponentiation.

For
N=N^{\prime}=1
, the function
U^{\mathbf{8}}_{(1,1)}(p_{H}^{+},p_{H}^{-})
is identical to the well-studied di-hemisphere invariant mass soft function, which appears in the di-hemisphere invariant mass distribution for dijet events in
e^{+}e^{-}
collisions
[
164
,
203
]
, except that it involves adjoint rather than fundamental Wilson lines, and hence will have
a color factor of
C_{A}=3
rather than
C_{F}=4/3
.
As explained in
section
5.2
, the evolution for the beam and soft function parts of
U
can be carried out independently, as shown by the orange and green dashed lines in
figure
11
.
Therefore, the LL solution for quasi-diffraction is:

\displaystyle U^{\mathbf{8}}_{(1,1)}(p_{H}^{+},p_{H}^{-})\Big|_{LL}

\displaystyle=N_{\textbf{8}}\,\delta(p_{H}^{+})\>\delta(p_{H}^{-})\>U^{\rm LL}\,,\qquad U^{\rm LL}=e^{K_{1}}\,e^{K_{2}}\,,

(5.7)

where
N_{\textbf{8}}
is a normalization factor from the color projection. These
\delta
-functions collapse the
dp_{g}^{+}dp_{g}^{-}
integrals in eq. (
4.1
).
The Sudakov kernels are

\displaystyle K_{1}

\displaystyle=K(\mu_{B},\mu_{u1})\,,\qquad\qquad K_{2}=K(\mu_{S},\mu_{u2})\,,

(5.8)

where at LL order,

\displaystyle K(\mu,\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi C_{A}}{\beta_{0}^{2}}\bigg[\frac{1}{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{0})}-\frac{1}{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}-\frac{1}{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{0})}\ln\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{\alpha_{s}(\mu_{0})}\bigg]\,,

(5.9)

with
\beta_{0}=11C_{A}/3-2n_{f}/3
for
n_{f}
light quarks.
Eq. (
5.9
) includes the leading double logarithm,
K(\mu,\mu_{0})=-C_{A}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{\pi}\ln^{2}(\mu/\mu_{0})+{\cal O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})
, plus the correct infinite tower of logarithms produced by the running coupling constant.
We have
K(\mu,\mu_{0})<0
, so summing these Sudakov logarithms always causes exponential suppression of quasi-diffraction.

Figure 12
:

Plot of the Sudakov factor that suppresses the quasi-diffractive ultrasoft-collinear function for representative phase space points and HERA kinematics for
s
and
\gamma
.
The left panel shows a smaller
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}=3
with a less stringent cut on the radiation in
Y
,
allowing for larger values of
-t
. The right plot shows
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}=5
, where only smaller values of
-t
are allowed to ensure that the forward scattered proton or its remnants remain in
Y
.

Choosing our canonical scales to be

\displaystyle\mu_{B}^{2}=m_{Y}^{2}-t,

\displaystyle\mu_{S}^{2}=Q^{2}+\frac{Q^{2}}{\beta},{}

\displaystyle\mu_{u1}^{2}=\frac{Q^{2}z^{2}}{xh^{2}}=\frac{e^{2\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}}(m_{Y}^{2}-t)^{2}}{\gamma^{2}s}\,,

\displaystyle\mu_{u2}^{2}=\frac{xh^{2}\,Q^{2}}{\beta^{2}}=\frac{x^{2}\gamma^{2}e^{-2\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}}\,s}{\beta^{2}}\,,

(5.10)

the large logarithms we resum are
\ln^{2}(\mu_{B}^{2}/\mu_{u1}^{2})\sim\ln^{2}[e^{2\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}}(m_{Y}^{2}-t)/(\gamma^{2}s)]
and
\ln^{2}(\mu_{S}^{2}/\mu_{u2}^{2})\sim\ln^{2}[x^{2}\gamma^{2}e^{-2\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}}s)/(\beta^{2}Q^{2})]
. The second one has double logarithms in
x
,
so the damping from the Sudakov exponents increases as
x
decreases.
Since we can only trust our Sudakov calculation for perturbative couplings,
\alpha_{s}(\mu_{i})\ll 1
, we include a profile which freezes out all scales
\mu_{i}
at
1\,{\rm GeV}
.

In
figure
12
we plot the leading Sudakov suppression from
U^{\rm LL}
in eq. (
5.7
), which corresponds to the leading damping of the color octet exchange channel.
The smallest
x
shown here corresponds to the kinematic limit
x_{\rm min}=Q^{2}/s
for HERA.
While the choice of kinematics and
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
impact the value of the damping,
it is clear that this quasi-diffractive background is
not
always strongly damped by
U
.
For example, the left panel of
figure
12
shows that for
x=0.01
,
U
damps quasi-diffraction by only
\sim
10%; indeed, it only drops to the
\sim
5% level near
x=x_{\rm min}\sim 0.004
.
The right panel of
figure
12
shows the Sudakov damping for a larger value of
\eta_{\rm cut}^{\rm lab}
, which is more in line with those used at HERA. Here we find more damping, but again, it plateaus at the 5-7% level for the smaller values of
x
. This occurs because the scale
\mu_{2}
hits the
1\,{\rm GeV}
threshold, where
\alpha_{s}(\mu_{2})
is frozen, and there is no increase in the perturbative suppression from the
(\mu_{S},\mu_{u2})
hierarchy. Sudakov exponentials are predictions based on perturbative QCD emission probabilities.
In this frozen coupling region there may be further suppression from nonperturbative effects (like the shape functions, whose form is known for the hemisphere
U
).

Typically, perturbative uncertainties enter from higher-order resummation, which at LL order may amount to as much as 30%, depending on the parameter choices. This provides strong motivation for improving the perturbative accuracy of our results in the future, along with considering shape function models for nonperturbative effects.

Of course, to determine the overall contamination of the color-singlet diffractive signal in a cross section by the color-nonsinglet quasi-diffractive background, we must consider information about the soft and beam factors as well, since schematically

\displaystyle\frac{F_{i}^{\rm D\,quasi}}{F_{i}^{\rm D\,diff}}=\frac{B^{\rm quasi}\otimes U\otimes S_{i}^{\rm quasi}}{B^{\rm diff}\otimes S_{i}^{\rm diff}}\,,

(5.11)

where exact expressions are given in eqs. (
4.1
) and (
4.1
).
In
section
7
, we combine the Sudakov suppression results from this section with perturbative soft function calculations in
section
6
to obtain a more accurate estimate contamination of the diffractive cross section by quasi-diffraction.

6
Soft Function in the Perturbative Regime

In this section, we perturbatively expand the soft function
S_{i}
in eq. (
4.86
) in
\alpha_{s}(\mu)
to understand (quasi-)diffraction at perturbative invariant mass scales
\mu^{2}\sim Q^{2},m_{X}^{2},-t\gg\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}
.
This large region of kinematic phase space provides powerful tests of the universality of hadronic dynamics in (quasi-)diffraction and probes the dependence of the factorization formulas in eqs. (
4.1
) and (
4.1
) on the choice of structure function
i=\{L,2,3,4\}
.
Interestingly, HERA analyses largely focused on nonperturbative kinematics with
-t\lesssim 1\,{\rm GeV}^{2}\sim\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}\ll Q^{2}
; in this case, we can still match the soft function onto the soft-collinear function at
\mu\sim Q
with perturbative hard coefficients
{\cal H}_{i}^{\kappa}
; see
section
4.2
.

In
section
6.1
, we begin by defining notation. We write
S_{i}
in terms of a squared amplitude and phase space integrals, a form that is valid to all orders in perturbation theory. Our main focus is to calculate
S_{i}
at tree level in the soft sector (
q\bar{q}
) with any number of Glauber exchanges, and we determine its amplitude in
section
6.2
.
Because a
q\bar{q}
state consists of color-singlet and octet components (
3\otimes\bar{3}=1\oplus 8
), our calculations probe both the singlet diffractive signal and the octet quasi-diffractive background. In
section
6.3
, we calculate the soft function with one perturbative Glauber exchange; this proceeds through the octet channel.
In
section
6.3.1
, we calculate the soft function with one perturbative Glauber exchange and
N
nonperturbative Glauber exchanges, proving that only octet exchanges contribute. Finally, in
section
6.4
, we discuss the leading color-singlet contributions to the soft function.

6.1
General setup and notation

When we perturbatively expand the soft function in eq. (
4.86
), we must consider diagrams with various numbers of Glauber exchanges and soft particles, which we categorize as:

\displaystyle S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{2\pi}\Big)^{k}\>S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R[k]}\,.

(6.1)

Here,
[k]
denotes the perturbative order in the soft sector and
(N,N^{\prime})
indicates the number of Glauber operators inserted on each side of the final-state cut (see
figure
10
). In eq. (
6.1
),
k=0
is tree level in the soft sector, with an outgoing soft
q\bar{q}
state;
k=1
has either an extra real emission with a
q\bar{q}g
state or a one-loop virtual correction to the
q\bar{q}
state; and so forth. The color channel of the exchange is
R=R_{B}^{N\!N^{\prime}}
. The requirement that
-t\gg\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}
implies that at least one Glauber exchange has a perturbative coupling. The soft function scales
\mu^{2}\sim Q^{2},m_{X}^{2},-t\gg\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}
set the expansion scale
\alpha_{s}(\mu)
.

Eq. (
6.1
) only explicitly writes
\alpha_{s}^{k}
factors emanating from the soft sector.
Each
S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R[k]}
also contains additional factors of
\alpha_{s}(\mu)
associated to the Glauber operators
{\cal O}_{s}^{q_{n}B}
and
{\cal O}_{s}^{g_{n}B}
from eq. (
3.14
) and their associated virtual loop diagrams; the scale
\mu
reflects the transverse momentum
\mu^{2}\sim\vec{\tau}_{j\perp}^{\,2}
of the corresponding Glauber exchange.
Each exchange is either perturbative (
\vec{\tau}_{j\perp}^{\,2}\gg\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}
) or nonperturbative (
\vec{\tau}_{j\perp}^{\,2}\sim\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}
).
That is, each
S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R[k]}
carries an implicit factor of
\alpha_{s}^{N+N^{\prime}}(\mu)
along with multiplicative radiative corrections associated to the Glauber potentials; see
figure
13
a,b for examples.
To factor out these radiative corrections, we write

\displaystyle S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R[k]}

\displaystyle=\hat{S}_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R[k]}\>\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\prod_{j=1}^{N+N^{\prime}}\Big[8\pi\alpha_{s}(\mu)\mu^{2\epsilon}\iota^{\epsilon}\,G(\tau_{j\perp},\mu)\Big]\,,

(6.2)

where
\hat{S}_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R[k]}
are perturbatively calculable functions and
G
encodes the radiative corrections, which may be perturbative or nonperturbative.
Note that
\iota=\exp(\gamma_{E})/(4\pi)
is a factor arising from using the
\overline{\rm MS}
scheme, and we define each
G
to depend on only one scale
\tau_{j\perp}
. The factors in square brackets are
\mu
-independent. We also note that Glauber collapse forces graphs like
figure
13
e to be zero.

a)
b)
c)
d)
e)
x

Figure 13
:
Examples of radiative corrections to the soft function. The real emission and virtual correction in graphs a) and b) are sensitive to the scales
Q
and
m_{X}
, while the Glauber potential radiative corrections in c) and d) are only sensitive to scales
\tau_{i\perp}^{2}
. Graph e) vanishes due to the Glauber collapse rule.

Phase space integrals.

To calculate
S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R}
from eq. (
6.1
),
we first massage eq. (
4.86
) into a form involving a phase space integral and a squared amplitude,

\displaystyle S_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R}(k_{s}^{-},\{\tau_{i\perp},\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime}\},Q,t)=\Bigl(\frac{Q}{n\cdot q}\Bigr)^{2}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\,\mu\nu}\>\sumint_{X_{s}}\int\![d\tilde{y}]\,[d\tilde{y}^{\prime}]\,{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d}\!z\>\ e^{\frac{i}{2}(y^{+}-y^{\prime+})k_{s}^{-}}\,e^{+iz\cdot q}\,{}

\displaystyle\ \times\big\langle 0\big|\bar{T}\,J_{s}^{\mu}(z)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{i=1}^{N}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{i}}\!(\tilde{y},-\tau_{i\perp})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}P_{NR_{B}}\big|X_{s}\big\rangle\big\langle X_{s}\big|P_{N^{\prime}R_{B^{\prime}}}T\,J_{s}^{\nu}(0)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{j}^{\prime}}\!(\tilde{y}^{\prime},-\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|0\big\rangle{}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{Q}{n\cdot q}\Bigr)^{2}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\,\mu\nu}\>\sumint_{X_{s}}\int\![d\tilde{y}]\,[d\tilde{y}^{\prime}]\,{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d}\!z\ e^{\frac{i}{2}(y^{+}-y^{\prime+})k_{s}^{-}}\,e^{+iz\cdot q}\,e^{-iz\cdot p_{Xs}}\,e^{-iz_{\perp}\cdot\sum_{i}\tau_{i\perp}}{}

\displaystyle\ \times\big\langle 0\big|\bar{T}\,J_{s}^{\mu}(0)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{i=1}^{N}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{i}}\!(\tilde{y}-\tilde{z},-\tau_{i\perp})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}P_{NR_{B}}\big|X_{s}\big\rangle\big\langle X_{s}\big|P_{N^{\prime}R_{B^{\prime}}}T\,J_{s}^{\nu}(0)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{j}^{\prime}}\!(\tilde{y}^{\prime},-\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\big|0\big\rangle{}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{Q}{n\cdot q}\Bigr)^{2}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\,\mu\nu}\sumint_{X_{s}}\delta^{d}(q+\check{\tau}-p_{X_{s}})\Big\langle 0\Big|\!\int\![d\tilde{y}]\>e^{i\tilde{y}\cdot\check{\tau}}\,\bar{T}\,J_{s}^{\mu}(0)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{i=1}^{N}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{i}}\!(\tilde{y},-\tau_{i\perp})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}P_{NR_{B}}\Big|X_{s}\Big\rangle{}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Big\langle X_{s}\Big|P_{N^{\prime}R_{B^{\prime}}}\!\int\![d\tilde{y}^{\prime}]\>e^{-i\tilde{y}^{\prime}\cdot\check{\tau}}\,T\,J_{s}^{\nu}(0)\Big\{\!\!\!\Big\{\!\text{\footnotesize$\prod_{j=1}^{N^{\prime}}$}\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{j}^{\prime}}\!(\tilde{y}^{\prime},-\tau_{j\perp}^{\prime})\!\!\Big\}\!\!\!\Big\}\Big|0\Big\rangle\,,

(6.27)

where as before
[d\tilde{y}]=dy^{+}dy^{-}/2
. To obtain the second equality, we shift the coordinates in the first matrix element by
-z
, and to obtain the last line we shift
\tilde{y}\to\tilde{y}+\tilde{z}
and perform the
{d\!\!{}^{-}}^{d}\!z
integral.
The final expression in eq. (
6.1
) makes clear that the soft function corresponds to a scattering cross section with incoming momenta
q
and
\check{\tau}
, where

\displaystyle\check{\tau}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}k_{s}^{-}n^{\mu}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tau_{i\perp}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}k_{s}^{-}n^{\mu}+\tau_{\perp}^{\mu}\,.

(6.28)

See
figure
9
for our sign conventions.
If uc radiation is not present then
\check{\tau}^{-}=k_{s}^{-}=\tau^{-}
and
\check{\tau}_{\perp}^{\mu}=\tau_{\perp}^{\mu}
; note that
\check{\tau}^{+}=0
.

For
N=N^{\prime}=1
, it is instructive to write the Glauber operators in position space,

\displaystyle S_{i(1,1)}^{8}=\Bigl(\frac{Q}{n\cdot q}\Bigr)^{2}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\,\mu\nu}\>\sumint_{X_{s}}\ \delta^{d}(q+\check{\tau}-p_{X_{s}})\ \Big\langle 0\Big|\!\int\!d^{d}y\>e^{iy\cdot\check{\tau}}\,\bar{T}\,J_{s}^{\mu}(0)\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{1}}\!(y)\Big|X_{s}\Big\rangle{}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Big\langle X_{s}\Big|\int\!d^{d}y^{\prime}\>e^{-iy^{\prime}\cdot\check{\tau}}\,T\,J_{s}^{\nu}(0)\mathcal{O}_{s}^{B_{1}^{\prime}}\!(y^{\prime})\Big|0\Big\rangle\,,

(6.37)

where
R=8
because a single gluon is always a color octet. Only the four-vector
\check{\tau}
appears because there is only one relevant transverse momentum
\tau_{1\perp}=-\tau_{\perp}=-\tau_{1\perp}^{\prime}
.

Following eq. (
6.2
), we introduce a notation for the reduced amplitudes after stripping off Glauber potential factors from eq. (
6.1
),

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R}=\Bigl(\frac{Q}{n\cdot q}\Bigr)^{2}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\,\mu\nu}\>\sumint_{X_{s}}\ (2\pi)^{d}\,\delta^{d}(q+\check{\tau}-p_{X_{s}})\>\text{Tr}\,\Big[{\cal M}_{N}^{\mu\{B_{i}\}\dagger}\>{\cal M}_{N^{\prime}}^{\nu\{B^{\prime}_{j}\}}\Big]\,,

traced over final-state spin and color indices. Like in eq. (
6.1
), we will sometimes add a superscript
[k]
to both
\hat{S}_{i(N,N^{\prime})}^{R}
and
{\cal M}_{N}^{\nu\{B_{j}\}}
to denote order in the soft sector.

6.2
Leading order soft amplitude with
N
Glaubers

Let us examine the soft function amplitude
{\cal M}_{N}^{\mu\{B_{i}\}[0]}
at lowest order (
q\bar{q}
) in the soft sector and with
N
Glaubers. As this section only considers the LO case, we drop superscripts
[0]
for concision.
Because
q\bar{q}
can only be in a
1
or
8
color representation, we project the amplitudes onto these two color channels using quark color indices
\alpha\beta\gamma\delta
as

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{N\alpha\beta}^{\mu\{B_{i}\}\dagger}\>\delta_{\alpha\gamma}\delta_{\beta\delta}\>{\cal M}_{N^{\prime}\gamma\delta}^{\nu\{B^{\prime}_{j}\}}

\displaystyle={\cal M}_{N\alpha\beta}^{\mu\{B_{i}\}\dagger}\>\Big(\frac{1}{N_{c}}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{\gamma\delta}+2(T^{C})_{\alpha\beta}(T^{C})_{\gamma\delta}\Big)\>{\cal M}_{N^{\prime}\gamma\delta}^{\nu\{B^{\prime}_{j}\}}{}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{N_{c}}{\cal M}_{N(1)}^{\mu\{B_{i}\}\dagger}\>{\cal M}_{N^{\prime}(1)}^{\nu\{B^{\prime}_{j}\}}+2{\cal M}_{N(8)}^{\mu\{B_{i}C\}\dagger}\>{\cal M}_{N^{\prime}(8)}^{\nu\{B^{\prime}_{j}C\}}\,.

(6.46)

This color projection onto the final
q\bar{q}
state differs from the soft color projection from the Glaubers in eq. (
4.73
).
While these two different color projections must have irreducible representations of the same dimension to obtain a color singlet, their overlap may take the form of a linear combination when one of the projections has more than one irrep of the same dimension.
For example, three Glaubers have eight octet irreps, and the single octet
q\bar{q}
state irrep projects onto a linear combination of these octet Glauber irreps.

Figure 14
:
The soft function amplitude at tree level in the soft sector (
q\bar{q}
, green solid lines) with one Glauber exchange (red dotted lines).

We start with the case
N=1
in
figure
14
, which has only an octet channel. We have

\displaystyle{\cal M}^{\nu}_{1(8)}=

\displaystyle e_{q}\,{\rm tr}[T^{C}T^{B_{1}^{\prime}}]\,\bar{u}(k_{1})\bigg[\gamma^{\nu}\frac{(\not{\tau}_{\!\perp}-\not{k}_{2})\frac{\not{n}}{2}}{(\tau-k_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\frac{\not{n}}{2}(\not{k}_{1}-\not{\tau}_{\!\perp})}{(k_{1}-\tau)^{2}}\gamma^{\nu}\bigg]v(-k_{2})

(6.47)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{q}\,T_{F}\delta^{CB_{1}^{\prime}}\bar{u}(k_{1})\bigg[-\gamma^{\nu}\frac{(n\cdot k_{2}-\not{\tau}_{\!\perp}\frac{\not{n}}{2})}{(-2\tau\cdot k_{2}+\tau^{2})}+\frac{(n\cdot k_{1}-\frac{\not{n}}{2}\not{\tau}_{\!\perp})}{(-2\tau\cdot k_{1}+\tau^{2})}\gamma^{\nu}\bigg]v(-k_{2}){}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{q}\,T_{F}\delta^{CB_{1}^{\prime}}\bar{u}(k_{1})\bigg[\gamma^{\nu}\frac{(1-\not{\tau}_{\!\perp}\frac{\not{n}}{2n\cdot k_{2}})}{\tau^{-}+\frac{\vec{\tau}_{\perp}^{\,2}}{n\cdot k_{2}}-\frac{2\vec{\tau}_{\perp}\cdot\vec{k}_{2\perp}}{n\cdot k_{2}}}-\frac{(1-\frac{\not{n}}{2n\cdot k_{1}}\not{\tau}_{\!\perp})}{\tau^{-}+\frac{\vec{\tau}_{\perp}^{\,2}}{n\cdot k_{1}}-\frac{2\vec{\tau}_{\perp}\cdot\vec{k}_{1\perp}}{n\cdot k_{1}}}\gamma^{\nu}\bigg]v(-k_{2})\,,{}

where we have stripped the prefactor
8\pi\alpha_{s}
as discussed above and color-stripped the spinors. Here,
e_{q}
is the quark electric charge. To obtain the second line, we use the on-shell equations of motion
\bar{u}(k_{1})\not{k}_{1}=\not{k}_{2}v(-k_{2})=0
and
k_{1}^{2}=k_{2}^{2}=0
.

Figure 15
:
The soft function amplitude at tree level in the soft sector (green solid lines) with two Glauber exchanges (red dotted lines).

Next, we consider
N=2
, which has both color-singlet and octet contributions. In
figure
15
, we see that four diagrams contribute. We find that

\displaystyle{\cal M}^{\nu}_{2(r)}

\displaystyle=e_{q}\,{\cal T}_{(r)a}\,\bar{u}(k_{1})\bigg\{\!\!\!\bigg\{\!\frac{\gamma^{\nu}(\not{k}_{2}\!-\!\not{\tau}_{\!\perp}\!)\frac{\not{n}}{2}(\not{k}_{2}\!-\!\not{\tau}_{\!2\perp}\!)\frac{\not{n}}{2}}{(k_{2}-\tau)^{2}\,(k_{2}-\tau_{2})^{2}}-\frac{\frac{\not{n}}{2}(\not{k}_{1}\!-\!\not{\tau}_{\!1\perp}\!)\gamma^{\nu}(\not{k}_{2}\!-\!\not{\tau}_{\!2\perp}\!)\frac{\not{n}}{2}}{(k_{1}-\tau_{1})^{2}\,(k_{2}-\tau_{2})^{2}}\!\bigg\}\!\!\!\bigg\}v(-k_{2})

(6.48)

\displaystyle-e_{q}\,{\cal T}_{(r)b}\,\bar{u}(k_{1})\bigg\{\!\!\!\bigg\{\!\frac{\frac{\not{n}}{2}(\not{k}_{1}\!-\!\not{\tau}_{\!2\perp}\!)\gamma^{\nu}(\not{k}_{2}\!-\!\not{\tau}_{\!1\perp}\!)\frac{\not{n}}{2}}{(k_{1}-\tau_{2})^{2}\,(k_{2}-\tau_{1})^{2}}-\frac{\frac{\not{n}}{2}(\not{k}_{1}\!-\!\not{\tau}_{\!2\perp}\!)\frac{\not{n}}{2}(\not{k}_{1}\!-\!\not{\tau}_{\!\perp}\!)\gamma^{\nu}}{(k_{1}-\tau_{2})^{2}\,(k_{1}-\tau)^{2}}\!\bigg\}\!\!\!\bigg\}v(-k_{2}){}

The first and last pairs of diagrams in
figure
15
each carry the same color factors

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{(1)a}

\displaystyle=T_{F}\delta^{B_{1}^{\prime}B_{2}^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{(8)a}

\displaystyle={\rm tr}[T^{C}T^{B_{1}^{\prime}}T^{B_{2}^{\prime}}]\,,{}

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{(1)b}

\displaystyle=T_{F}\delta^{B_{1}^{\prime}B_{2}^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{(8)b}

\displaystyle={\rm tr}[T^{C}T^{B_{2}^{\prime}}T^{B_{1}^{\prime}}]\,,

(6.49)

where the subscripts
a
and
b
indicate color factors appearing for
N=2
.
To simplify eq. (
6.48
), we carry out the Glauber collapse operation
\{\!\!\{\cdots\}\!\!\}
, which sets
\tau_{1}^{\pm}=\tau^{\pm}-\tau_{2}^{\pm}
and requires integrating over
\tau_{2}^{\pm}
.
To perform the integrations properly, we must include collinear propagators that arise from Glauber interactions in the beam function. However, the value of
\mathcal{M}
is independent of which collinear propagators the Glaubers attach to in the beam function. Thus,

\displaystyle{\cal M}^{\nu}_{2(r)}

\displaystyle=e_{q}\,{\cal T}_{(r)a}\,\bar{u}(k_{1})\Bigg[\!-\frac{\gamma^{\nu}\Bigl(1\!-\!\frac{\not{\tau}_{\!\perp}\not{n}}{2k_{2}^{+}}\Bigr)}{\tau^{-}\!+\!\frac{\vec{\tau}_{\perp}^{\,2}}{k_{2}^{+}}\!-\!\frac{2\vec{\tau}_{\perp}\cdot\vec{k}_{2\perp}}{k_{2}^{+}}}+\frac{\Bigl(1\!-\!\frac{\not{n}\not{\tau}_{\!1\perp}}{2k_{1}^{+}}\!\Bigr)\gamma^{\nu}\Bigl(1\!-\!\frac{\not{\tau}_{\!2\perp}\not{n}}{2k_{2}^{+}}\!\Bigr)}{\tau^{-}\!+\!\frac{\vec{\tau}_{1\perp}^{\,2}}{k_{1}^{+}}\!-\!\frac{2\vec{\tau}_{1\perp}\cdot\vec{k}_{1\perp}}{k_{1}^{+}}\!+\!\frac{\vec{\tau}_{2\perp}^{\,2}}{k_{2}^{+}}\!-\!\frac{2\vec{\tau}_{2\perp}\cdot\vec{k}_{2\perp}}{k_{2}^{+}}}\Bigg]\!v(-k_{2}){}

\displaystyle\hskip-15.6491pt+e_{q}\,{\cal T}_{(r)b}\,\bar{u}(k_{1})\Bigg[\!\frac{\Bigl(1\!-\!\frac{\not{n}\not{\tau}_{\!2\perp}}{2k_{1}^{+}}\!\Bigr)\gamma^{\nu}\Bigl(1\!-\!\frac{\not{\tau}_{\!1\perp}\not{n}}{2k_{2}^{+}}\!\Bigr)}{\tau^{-}\!+\!\frac{\vec{\tau}_{2\perp}^{\,2}}{k_{1}^{+}}\!-\!\frac{2\vec{\tau}_{2\perp}\cdot\vec{k}_{1\perp}}{k_{1}^{+}}\!+\!\frac{\vec{\tau}_{1\perp}^{\,2}}{k_{2}^{+}}\!-\!\frac{2\vec{\tau}_{1\perp}\cdot\vec{k}_{2\perp}}{k_{2}^{+}}}-\frac{(1\!-\!\frac{\not{n}}{2n\cdot k_{1}}\not{\tau}_{\!\perp}\!)\gamma^{\nu}}{\tau^{-}\!+\!\frac{\vec{\tau}_{\perp}^{\,2}}{k_{1}^{+}}\!-\!\frac{2\vec{\tau}_{\perp}\cdot\vec{k}_{1\perp}}{k_{1}^{+}}}\Bigg]\!v(-k_{2})\,.

(6.50)

When we collapse the two Glaubers, a factor of
1/(2!)
arises. We choose to group this factor into the
\perp
-convolution between the soft and beam functions in eq. (
4.41
), and hence it does not appear in eq. (
6.2
).

Figure 16
:
Soft function at tree level in the soft sector (
q\bar{q}
, green lines) and at arbitrary Glauber order
N
(red dotted). Each Glauber gluon may attach to either the quark or the anti-quark. We say that a diagram is planar when all Glaubers attach to the same particle in the soft sector (right), and nonplanar when they attach to different soft particles (left).

The soft amplitude with a
q\bar{q}
soft sector and
N
Glaubers has contributions from a multitude of diagrams, as shown in
figure
16
. We carry out full calculations in
appendix
A.2
and present the result here:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\nu}_{N(r)}=e_{q}\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{N_{q}}\frac{\bar{u}(k_{1})\Big(1-\frac{\not{n}}{2n\cdot k_{1}}\not{\tau}_{\sigma_{q}\perp}\Big)\gamma^{\nu}\Big(1-\not{\tau}_{\sigma_{\bar{q}}\perp}\frac{\not{n}}{2n\cdot k_{2}}\Big)\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{(r)}\,v(-k_{2})}{\tau^{-}+\frac{1}{n\cdot k_{1}}(\vec{\tau}_{\sigma_{q}\perp}-2\vec{k}_{1\perp})\cdot\vec{\tau}_{\sigma_{q}\perp}+\frac{1}{n\cdot k_{2}}(\vec{\tau}_{\sigma_{\bar{q}}\perp}-2\vec{k}_{2\perp})\cdot\vec{\tau}_{\sigma_{\bar{q}}\perp}}\,.

(6.51)

Here, the notation
\sigma_{X}
indicates permutations of Glauber attachments to each particle
X=\{q,\bar{q}\}
in the soft sector. For example, for
N=5
Glaubers we have
2^{5}
permutations for attachments to
q\bar{q}
, which include cases like
\sigma=\{\sigma_{q},\sigma_{\bar{q}}\}=\big\{\{1,3,4\},\{2,5\}\big\}
and
\sigma=\big\{\emptyset,\{1,2,3,4,5\}\big\}
. Note that for consistency, we always number Glauber momenta
\tau_{i}
from left to right, as in
figure
16
. We let
N_{X}
denote the number of elements in
\sigma_{X}
. The total momentum of the particles in
\sigma_{X}
is then
\tau_{\sigma_{X}}=\sum_{i\in\sigma_{X}}\tau_{i}
. The singlet and octet color matrices for each
\sigma
are

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{(1)}

\displaystyle={\rm tr}\Big[T^{A({\sigma_{q}^{\mbox{\tiny$N_{q}$}}})}\cdots T^{A({\sigma_{q}^{2}})}\,T^{A(\sigma_{q}^{1})}\ T^{A({\sigma_{\bar{q}}^{1}})}\,T^{A(\sigma_{\bar{q}}^{2})}\cdots T^{A(\sigma_{\bar{q}}^{\mbox{\tiny$N_{\bar{q}}$}})}\Big]\,,{}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\sigma}_{(8)}

\displaystyle={\rm tr}\Big[T^{C}\,T^{A(\sigma_{q}^{\mbox{\tiny$N_{q}$}})}\cdots T^{A(\sigma_{q}^{2})}\,T^{A(\sigma_{q}^{1})}\ T^{A(\sigma_{\bar{q}}^{1})}\,T^{A(\sigma_{\bar{q}}^{2})}\cdots T^{A(\sigma_{\bar{q}}^{\mbox{\tiny$N_{\bar{q}}$}})}\Big]\,.

(6.52)

Here
\sigma_{X}^{i}
indicates element
i
in
\sigma_{X}
.
T^{A(\sigma_{X}^{i})}
is the color matrix for the corresponding Glauber attachment. Note that sometimes different permutations
\sigma
have identical color factors, which is why eq. (
6.2
) has a more complicated notation than eq. (
6.2
).

6.3
One perturbative Glauber (quasi-diffraction)

At leading soft order (
q\bar{q}
) and one perturbative Glauber exchange, the soft function is

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{i(1,1)}^{8[0]}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\mu\nu}\Bigl(\frac{Q}{n\cdot q}\Bigr)^{2}\int d\Phi_{2}^{d}\>2\,\mathcal{M}_{1(8)}^{\mu\dagger}\mathcal{M}_{1(8)}^{\nu}

(6.53)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\mu\nu}\Bigl(\frac{Q}{n\cdot q}\Bigr)^{2}\int[d^{d}k_{1}]_{+}[d^{d}k_{2}]_{+}(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{d}(q+\tau-k_{1}-k_{2})\,2\,\mathcal{M}_{1(8)}^{\mu\dagger}\mathcal{M}_{1(8)}^{\nu}{}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\mu\nu}\ \hat{S}_{(1,1)}^{8[0]\mu\nu}{}\,,

where we have used
\sumint_{X_{s}}=\int d\Phi_{2}^{d}
to obtain an integral over two-body phase space,
[d^{d}k_{i}]_{+}=d^{d}k_{i}\delta(k_{i}^{2})\theta(k_{i}^{0})/(2\pi)^{d-1}
.
Also recall that
n\cdot\tau=0
for the soft function; see
section
4
.
Note that we use
[0]
in the soft function, but we continue dropping
[0]
from
\mathcal{M}
. To simplify the calculation, we use the tensor structure decomposition from eq. (
2.47
):

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{(1,1)}^{8[0]\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}w_{i}^{\prime\mu\nu}\hat{S}_{i(1,1)}^{8[0]}\,.

(6.54)

Here, we use
w_{i}^{\prime\mu\nu}
rather than
w_{i}^{\mu\nu}
because
the soft function is
x
-independent.
(This decomposition also applies for higher-order soft functions and any
N
and
N^{\prime}
.)

Some structures
w_{i}^{\prime\mu\nu}
are defined in terms of
p^{\mu}
(via
U^{\mu}
). While
p^{\mu}
cannot directly enter
S_{i}
, as its only incoming momenta are
q^{\mu}
and
\tau^{\mu}
,
S_{i}
also depends on
n^{\mu}
through its soft Glauber operators. Using
p^{\mu}=\bar{n}\cdot p\,n^{\mu}/2=Q^{2}n^{\mu}/(2xn\cdot q)
, we write

\displaystyle U^{\mu}=\frac{Q}{n\cdot q}\left(n^{\mu}-\frac{n\cdot q}{q^{2}}q^{\mu}\right)\,,

(6.55)

which is an allowed basis element for the soft function.
Note that in the Breit frame,
x\bar{n}\cdot p/Q=Q/n\cdot q=1
. By RPI invariance,
S
is the same in any frame related to the Breit frame by a boost along the
n
-
\bar{n}
axis.

Our calculations will use dimensional regularization
d=4-2\epsilon

for both matrix elements and phase space integrals, so to work with the structures
w_{i}^{\prime\mu\nu}
we also need
d
-dimensional versions of the projection operators in eq. (
2.47
) and eq. (
2.53
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\prime\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{d-3}\left[g^{\mu\nu}+(5-2d)U^{\mu}U^{\nu}-(d-4)X^{\mu}X^{\nu}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{L}^{\prime\mu\nu}=2\,U^{\mu}U^{\nu}\,,

(6.56)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{3}^{\prime\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{d-3}\,\left[g^{\mu\nu}-U^{\mu}U^{\nu}+(d-2)X^{\mu}X^{\nu}\right]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{4}^{\prime\mu\nu}=-\,(U^{\mu}X^{\nu}+X^{\mu}U^{\nu})\,,{}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{4A}^{\prime\mu\nu}=i\,(U^{\mu}X^{\nu}-X^{\mu}U^{\nu})\,.

These projectors obey
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{i\mu\nu}w_{j}^{\prime\mu\nu}=\delta_{ij}
. Note that the five additional structure functions associated with a polarized hadron beam affect only the beam functions and therefore share the same soft functions.

The soft matrix elements in eq. (
6.53
) are simply those for
\gamma^{*}(q)g(\tau)\to q(k_{1})\bar{q}(k_{2})
. We use back-to-back kinematics for the photon and gluon, and parametrize their momenta as

\displaystyle q=(q_{0},0,0,-\tau_{z})\,,

\displaystyle\tau=(\tau_{0},0,0,\tau_{z})\,,

\displaystyle k_{1}=(k_{0},k_{0}\sin\theta,0,k_{0}\cos\theta)\,,

\displaystyle k_{2}=(k_{0},-k_{0}\sin\theta,0,-k_{0}\cos\theta)\,,

\displaystyle n=(n_{0},|n_{\perp}|\cos\phi,|n_{\perp}|\sin\phi,n_{z})\,.

(6.57)

Here
n^{\mu}
from the Breit frame
transforms as a four-vector to leave dot products like
n\cdot\tau
and
n\cdot k_{1}
fixed. We take the outgoing quarks to be back-to-back in the
x
-
z
plane, so that only
n^{\mu}
depends on the azimuthal angle
\phi
.
The momentum components in eq. (
6.3
) are

\displaystyle q_{0}=\frac{Q}{2}\frac{1-2\beta}{\sqrt{\beta(1-\beta+\beta\frac{t}{Q^{2}})}}\,,

\displaystyle\tau_{0}=\frac{Q}{2}\frac{1+2\beta\frac{t}{Q^{2}}}{\sqrt{\beta(1-\beta+\beta\frac{t}{Q^{2}})}}\,,

\displaystyle\tau_{z}=\frac{Q}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1+4\beta^{2}\frac{t}{Q^{2}}}{\beta(1-\beta+\beta\frac{t}{Q^{2}})}}\,,

\displaystyle n_{0}=\frac{n\cdot q}{Q}\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{1-\beta+\beta\frac{t}{Q^{2}}}}\,,

\displaystyle k_{0}=\frac{Q}{2}\sqrt{\frac{1-\beta+\beta\frac{t}{Q^{2}}}{\beta}}\,,

\displaystyle n_{z}=n_{0}\frac{1+2\beta\frac{t}{Q^{2}}}{\sqrt{1+4\beta^{2}\frac{t}{Q^{2}}}}\,,{}

\displaystyle|n_{\perp}|=\frac{n\cdot q}{Q}\frac{\sqrt{-t}}{Q}\frac{2\beta}{\sqrt{1+4\beta^{2}\frac{t}{Q^{2}}}}\,.

(6.58)

Next, the phase space factor in eq. (
6.53
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\int\!\!d\Phi_{2}^{d}

\displaystyle=\int[d^{d}k_{1}]_{+}[d^{d}k_{2}]_{+}(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{d}(q+\tau-k_{1}-k_{2})=\frac{(4\pi)^{\epsilon}}{32\pi^{2}}\frac{k_{0}^{-2\epsilon}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\int\!\!\frac{d\cos\theta\,d\phi}{(1-\cos^{2}\theta)^{\epsilon}}\,.

(6.59)

Carrying out the
\theta
and
\phi
integrals, we can evaluate eq. (
6.53
)
to obtain

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{i(1,1)}^{8[0]}=\delta^{AB}T_{F}\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\beta^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}}{(1-4\beta^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2})^{4}}\sum_{j}e_{j}^{2}\bigg(\!\hat{S}_{i,\rm{r}}\!+\!\hat{S}_{i,\rm{t}}\frac{\text{tanh}^{-1}\sqrt{1-4\beta^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}}}{\sqrt{1-4\beta^{2}\lambda_{t}^{2}}}\bigg),

(6.60)

where
j
sums over quark and anti-quark flavors in the fermion loop, and the terms
\hat{S}_{i,r/t}
capture the rational (
r
) and transcendental (
t
) terms in the soft function:

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{L,\rm{r}}=

\displaystyle 4\beta(1-\beta)+4\beta(-47\beta^{3}+72\beta^{2}-30\beta+1)\lambda_{t}^{2}

(6.61)

\displaystyle-4\beta^{2}(6\beta^{4}-116\beta^{3}+180\beta^{2}-72\beta+1)\lambda_{t}^{4}-4\beta^{4}(60\beta^{2}-116\beta+47)\lambda_{t}^{6}-24\beta^{6}\lambda_{t}^{8}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{L,\rm{t}}=

\displaystyle 8\beta^{2}(18\beta^{2}-24\beta+7)\lambda_{t}^{2}+48\beta^{3}(6\beta^{3}-18\beta^{2}+17\beta-4)\lambda_{t}^{4}{}

\displaystyle-48\beta^{4}(2\beta^{4}+4\beta^{3}-18\beta^{2}+18\beta-3)\lambda_{t}^{6}+32\beta^{6}(2\beta^{2}-6\beta+9)\lambda_{t}^{8}-96\beta^{8}\lambda_{t}^{10}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{2,\rm{r}}=

\displaystyle-2(6\beta^{2}-6\beta+1)-6\beta(39\beta^{3}-64\beta^{2}+28\beta-2)\lambda_{t}^{2}{}

\displaystyle-12\beta^{2}(11\beta^{4}-66\beta^{3}+90\beta^{2}-32\beta+1)\lambda_{t}^{4}-2\beta^{4}(212\beta^{2}-396\beta+117)\lambda_{t}^{6}-132\beta^{6}\lambda_{t}^{8}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{2,\rm{t}}=

\displaystyle 2(2\beta^{2}-2\beta+1)+4\beta(48\beta^{3}-66\beta^{2}+17\beta-1)\lambda_{t}^{2}{}

\displaystyle+4\beta^{2}(108\beta^{4}-324\beta^{3}+318\beta^{2}-66\beta+1)\lambda_{t}^{4}-16\beta^{4}(\beta^{4}+26\beta^{3}-73\beta^{2}+81\beta-12)\lambda_{t}^{6}{}

\displaystyle+16\beta^{6}(22\beta^{2}-26\beta+27)\lambda_{t}^{8}-16\beta^{8}\lambda_{t}^{10}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{3,\rm{r}}=

\displaystyle-\beta^{2}-4\beta^{2}(18\beta^{2}-25\beta+6)\lambda_{t}^{2}+\beta^{2}(-116\beta^{4}+440\beta^{3}-468\beta^{2}+100\beta-1)\lambda_{t}^{4}{}

\displaystyle-8\beta^{4}(33\beta^{2}-55\beta+9)\lambda_{t}^{6}-116\beta^{6}\lambda_{t}^{8}\,,{}

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{3,\rm{t}}=

\displaystyle 8\beta^{2}(6\beta^{2}-6\beta+1)\lambda_{t}^{2}+24\beta^{3}(9\beta^{3}-24\beta^{2}+16\beta-2)\lambda_{t}^{4}{}

\displaystyle+48\beta^{4}(\beta^{4}-6\beta^{3}+18\beta^{2}-12\beta+1)\lambda_{t}^{6}-8\beta^{6}(4\beta^{2}+36\beta-27)\lambda_{t}^{8}+48\beta^{8}\lambda_{t}^{10}\,,{}

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{4,\rm{r}}=

\displaystyle 2\beta(25\beta^{2}-28\beta+6)\lambda_{t}+4\beta^{2}(55\beta^{3}-138\beta^{2}+96\beta-14)\lambda_{t}^{3}{}

\displaystyle+2\beta^{3}(-128\beta^{3}+396\beta^{2}-276\beta+25)\lambda_{t}^{5}+4\beta^{5}(55-64\beta)\lambda_{t}^{7}\,,{}

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{4,\rm{t}}=

\displaystyle-4\beta(6\beta^{2}-6\beta+1)\lambda_{t}+24\beta^{2}(-12\beta^{3}+22\beta^{2}-11\beta+1)\lambda_{t}^{3}{}

\displaystyle-24\beta^{3}(6\beta^{4}-36\beta^{3}+54\beta^{2}-22\beta+1)\lambda_{t}^{5}-32\beta^{5}(13\beta^{2}-27\beta+9)\lambda_{t}^{7}-144\beta^{7}\lambda_{t}^{9}\,,{}

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{4A,\rm{r}}=

\displaystyle\,0\,,{}

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{4A,\rm{t}}=

\displaystyle\,0\,,{}

where as usual,
\lambda_{t}=\sqrt{-t}/Q
.
For
N=N^{\prime}=1
one finds that
\hat{S}_{(1,1)}^{8[0]\mu\nu}
is symmetric in
\mu\nu
, and hence

S^{8[0]}_{4A(1,1)}
vanishes.

Case of
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
.

So far, we have considered the one-Glauber soft function for
\lambda\ll 1
and
\lambda_{t}\sim 1
.
To study the soft function for
\lambda,\lambda_{t}\ll 1
, we restore the appropriate factors of
\alpha_{s}
and
\pi
from eq. (
6.2
) into eq. (
6.60
) and expand in
\lambda_{t}=\sqrt{-t}/Q\ll 1
, giving:

\displaystyle S_{L(1,1)}^{8[0]}

\displaystyle=\delta^{AB}T_{F}\frac{-t}{Q^{2}}\frac{\beta^{2}}{\pi}\frac{[8\pi\alpha_{s}(\mu)]^{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\sum_{j}e_{j}^{2}\>4\beta(1-\beta)\times[1+\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{t})]\,,

(6.62)

\displaystyle S_{2(1,1)}^{8[0]}

\displaystyle=\delta^{AB}T_{F}\frac{-t}{Q^{2}}\frac{\beta^{2}}{\pi}\frac{[8\pi\alpha_{s}(\mu)]^{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\sum_{j}e_{j}^{2}\,{}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left[-(1-2\beta+2\beta^{2})\ln\left(\frac{-t}{Q^{2}}\beta^{2}\right)-2(1-6\beta+6\beta^{2})\right]\times[1+\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{t})]\,,

\displaystyle S_{3(1,1)}^{8[0]}

\displaystyle=\delta^{AB}T_{F}\frac{-t}{Q^{2}}\frac{\beta^{2}}{\pi}\frac{[8\pi\alpha_{s}(\mu)]^{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\sum_{j}e_{j}^{2}\>(-\beta^{2})\times[1+\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{t})]\,,

\displaystyle S_{4(1,1)}^{8[0]}

\displaystyle=\delta^{AB}T_{F}\left(\frac{-t}{Q}\right)^{3/2}\frac{\beta^{2}}{\pi}\frac{[8\pi\alpha_{s}(\mu)]^{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\sum_{j}e_{j}^{2}\,{}

\displaystyle\qquad\times 2\beta\left[(1-6\beta+6\beta^{2})\ln\left(\frac{-t}{Q^{2}}\beta^{2}\right)+6-28\beta+25\beta^{2}\right]\times[1+\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{t})]\,.

Interestingly,
S_{L,2,3}
have
\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{t}^{2})
contributions, whereas
S_{4}
starts at
\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{t}^{3})
. We do not consider
S_{4A(1,1)}^{8[0]}
, which is zero at this order even before the expansion in
\lambda_{t}
.
Because the soft function is dimensionless, it can only depend on
\lambda_{t}=\sqrt{-t}/Q
through a single logarithm (or power), while its
\beta
-dependence is less constrained. For
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
, our fixed-order expression in eq. (
6.62
) develops large logarithms
\ln(-t/Q^{2})
that require the further hard-collinear factorization from
section
4.2
, and then DGLAP resummation.

Hard-collinear factorization for
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
.

The
\lambda_{t}
-refactorization in eq. (
4.114
) refactorizes
S_{i}
as the convolution of a hard and a soft-collinear (sc) function.
14
14

14

Recall that this
\lambda_{t}
-refactorization is valid even for
-t\sim\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{2}
, as long as
Q^{2}\gg-t
. In this case, the soft-collinear function becomes a nonperturbative function and the perturbative partonic calculations carried out in this section still suffice to determine the
{\cal H}_{i}
, but not
S_{\rm c(1,1)}
.

In the
q\bar{q}
case, either both particles can carry
\lambda_{t}
-hard scaling, or one is
\lambda_{t}
-hard and the other is
\lambda_{t}
-collinear; see eq. (
4.2
). Thus, for
i=L,2,3
we can write eq. (
4.114
) as
15
15

15

In eq. (
6.62
), we observe that
S_{4}
is subleading in
\lambda_{t}
; therefore, the leading
\lambda_{t}
hard-collinear factorization described by the soft-collinear function here does not necessarily describe it.

\displaystyle S_{i(1,1)}^{8[0]}\Bigl(\frac{Q^{2}}{\beta}

\displaystyle,Q,t,\mu,\frac{\nu}{Q/\beta}\Bigr)=\frac{\beta^{2}}{Q^{2}}\Big[\mathcal{H}_{i}^{q(0)}\otimes_{-\>}S^{q;8(2)}_{\rm c(1,1)}+\mathcal{H}_{i}^{g(1)}\otimes_{-\>}S^{g;8(1)}_{\rm c(1,1)}\Big]\times[1+\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{t})]

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta^{2}}{Q^{2}}\int_{\beta}^{1}\frac{d\zeta}{\zeta}\>\bigg[\mathcal{H}^{q(0)}_{i}\Bigl(\frac{\beta}{\zeta},Q,\mu\Bigr)\>S^{q;8(2)}_{\rm c(1,1)}\left(\zeta,t,\frac{\nu}{Q/\beta},\mu\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\mathcal{H}^{g(1)}_{i}\Bigl(\frac{\beta}{\zeta},Q,\mu\Bigr)\>S^{g;8(1)}_{\rm c(1,1)}\left(\zeta,t,\frac{\nu}{Q/\beta},\mu\right)\bigg]\times[1+\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{t})]\,.

(6.63)

S_{i(1,1)}^{8[0]}
starts at
\alpha_{s}^{2}
,
and taking
\lambda_{t}\ll 1
factorizes it into quark and gluon terms whose organization of
\alpha_{s}
factors differs.
For the quark,
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{q}\sim\alpha_{s}^{0}
and
S_{c}^{q}\sim\alpha_{s}^{2}
, whereas for the gluon,
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{g}\sim\alpha_{s}
and
S_{c}^{g}\sim\alpha_{s}
. This mirrors the usual convention for hard functions in inclusive DIS.

We can determine the first term on the RHS of eq. (
6.3
) by expanding the matrix elements and two-body phase space in eq. (
6.59
) when either
q
or
\bar{q}
has collinear-
\lambda_{t}
scaling,
and including the
\overline{\rm MS}
counterterm for the
P_{qg}
splitting function:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{i}^{q(0)}\otimes_{-}S^{q;8(2)}_{\rm c(1,1)}

(6.64)

\displaystyle\ \ =\begin{cases}0\,,&i\!=\!L,3\\
\delta^{AB}T_{F}\frac{(-t)}{\pi}\frac{(8\pi\alpha_{s}(\mu))^{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\sum e_{j}^{2}\left[(1-2\beta+2\beta^{2})\ln\frac{\mu^{2}}{(1-\beta)\beta(-t)}-(1-2\beta)^{2}\right]\,,&i=2\\
\end{cases}\,.

We must be careful with the
\epsilon
-dependence in the
\overline{\rm MS}
factors,
[\alpha_{s}(\mu)\mu^{2\epsilon}\iota^{\epsilon}]^{2}
. To mirror the usual
choice for hard functions in inclusive DIS, we expand the
\epsilon
dependence of one of these two factors, while keeping the other unexpanded together with
the
(2\pi)^{-2\epsilon}
from the Glauber potential normalization
(2\pi)^{d}
in eq. (
6.2
).
16
16

16

We can trace this extra
(2\pi)^{d}
to the difference between the hadronic tensor definitions in DIS and diffraction in eq. (
2.2
), where the diffractive case lacks a
(2\pi)^{d}
.

Different choices affect each term on the RHS of eq. (
6.3
), but not their overall sum on the LHS.

The leading-order soft-collinear function
S^{j;8[1]}_{\rm c(1,1)}
occurs for
j=g
and is expected to be
\delta_{jg}\delta(1-\zeta)
up to a normalization. This overall factor is fixed by the normalization choice in the operator definition of the soft-collinear function. Here, we take

\displaystyle S^{j;8(1)}_{\rm c(1,1)}=\delta^{AB}\,\frac{(8\pi)^{2}\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{(2\pi)^{4}}(-t)2\delta_{jg}\delta(1-\zeta)\,.

(6.65)

From these results, we can use eqs. (
6.62
) and (
6.3
) to derive that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{g(1)}_{L}(\zeta,Q,\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\frac{\alpha_{s}T_{F}e_{j}^{2}}{2\pi}4\zeta(1-\zeta)\,,

(6.66)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{g(1)}_{2}(\zeta,Q,\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\frac{\alpha_{s}T_{F}e_{j}^{2}}{2\pi}\left[(1-2\zeta+2\zeta^{2})\ln\frac{Q^{2}(1-\zeta)}{\mu^{2}\zeta}-1+8\zeta-8\zeta^{2}\right]\,,{}

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{g(1)}_{3}(\zeta,Q,\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\frac{\alpha_{s}T_{F}e_{j}^{2}}{2\pi}(-\zeta^{2})\,.{}

As expected from
section
4.2
, the hard coefficients
{\cal H}_{2,L}^{g}
are identical to those of DIS. We also present a new hard coefficient
{\cal H}_{3}^{g}
. We expect that the number of Glaubers
(N,N^{\prime})
and color channel do not affect these results, as the
\lambda_{t}
-refactorization is insensitive to them.

We leave to future work an independent perturbative computation of the soft-collinear function from its operator definition. Here, we instead take as input the known DIS coefficients
{\cal H}_{2,L}^{q[0]}=H_{2,L}^{q[0]}
from eq. (
4.2
), and derive the form of the soft-collinear function from eq. (
6.64
):

\displaystyle S^{q;8(2)}_{\rm c(1,1)}(\zeta,t,\mu)=\delta^{AB}T_{F}\frac{(-t)}{\pi}\frac{[8\pi\alpha_{s}(\mu)]^{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\left[(1-2\zeta+2\zeta^{2})\ln\frac{\mu^{2}}{(1-\zeta)\zeta(-t)}-\zeta(1-2\zeta)^{2}\right]\,.

(6.67)

This NLO soft-collinear function corresponds to the operator definition choice that yields the normalization in eq. (
6.65
) for its LO expression.
We see that the soft function logarithm
\ln\big(\frac{-t}{Q^{2}}\beta^{2}\big)
in eq. (
6.62
)
gets decomposed into
\ln\big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{Q^{2}(1-\beta)/\beta}\big)
in the hard function in eq. (
6.66
) and

\ln\big(\frac{\mu^{2}}{-t\beta(1-\beta)}\big)
in the soft-collinear function in eq. (
6.67
).
Since the standard DIS hard functions obey the DGLAP renormalization group equations, we see that the
\lambda_{t}
-refactorization resums large DGLAP logarithms
\ln(-t/Q^{2})=\ln\lambda_{t}^{2}
as anticipated.

6.3.1
One perturbative and
N-1
nonperturbative Glaubers

We now consider diagrams with a single perturbative Glauber and an arbitrary number
(N-1)
of nonperturbative Glauber exchanges, which have
\tau_{i\perp}\sim\Lambda_{\rm QCD}
and
\alpha_{s}\sim 1
.
These are important contributions as nothing suppresses them in principle, unlike contributions with additional perturbative Glaubers that are suppressed by factors
\alpha_{s}\ll 1
.
We will see that these contributions do not spoil the predictive power of our leading-order soft function calculation.
This analysis corresponds to carrying out a
\lambda_{\Lambda}\ll 1
expansion for the soft function.

To assist in our calculations, let us define some notation. As introduced in
section
6.2
, we label Glauber momenta
\tau_{i}
in order from left to right, and we classify diagrams by the permutation
\sigma=\{\sigma_{q},\sigma_{\bar{q}}\}
indicating which Glaubers
i
attach to the quark and anti-quark lines, respectively. In this section, we tack an extra subscript
p
onto each
\sigma_{p}
that indicates which of the
N
Glauber gluons is perturbative.
The key expansion that we can exploit to simplify the perturbative soft amplitudes in the presence of nonperturbative Glaubers is
\tau_{j,\perp}\ll\tau_{p,\perp}
for
j\neq p
, while
\tau_{\perp}\simeq\tau_{p,\perp}
.
There are two qualitatively different types of diagrams: those with
p=1
and those with
p\neq 1
.

Vanishing of
p\neq 1
diagrams.

Let us discuss
p\neq 1
first, considering an arbitrary permutation
\sigma_{p\neq 1}=\{\sigma_{q},\sigma_{\bar{q}}\}
that attaches the perturbative
p
to the quark line.
Expanding in
\tau_{j,\perp}\ll\tau_{p,\perp}
,
we can write the soft amplitude as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{N}^{\sigma_{p\neq 1}}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{N_{q}-1}\bar{u}(k_{1})\frac{\not{n}}{2}\frac{(\not{k}_{1}-\not{\tau}_{\perp})}{(k_{1}-\tau)^{2}}\gamma^{\mu}

(6.68)

\displaystyle\times T^{A(\sigma_{q}^{N_{q}})}T^{A(\sigma_{q}^{N_{q}-1})}\cdots T^{A_{p}}\cdots T^{A(\sigma_{q}^{2})}T^{A(\sigma_{q}^{1})}T^{A({\sigma_{\bar{q}}^{1}})}\,T^{A(\sigma_{\bar{q}}^{2})}\cdots T^{A(\sigma_{\bar{q}}^{\mbox{\tiny$N_{\bar{q}}$}})}v(-k_{2})\,,{}

where
A_{p}
is the color index for the perturbative Glauber.
Because the first Glauber is nonperturbative, we can consider how eq. (
6.68
) differs
when that Glauber attaches to the quark or antiquark lines, as shown in
figure
17
.
These two amplitudes are identical except for the change of
(-1)^{N_{q}-1}\to(-1)^{N_{q}}
.
Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma_{p\neq 1}}\mathcal{M}_{N}^{\sigma_{p\neq 1}}=0\,.

(6.69)

It is straightforward to see that this statement holds for all
N
, arbitrary color projections, and regardless of whether
p
attaches to the quark or anti-quark.
Therefore, the lowest-order soft function vanishes for one perturbative Glauber inserted
after
one or more nonperturbative Glauber exchanges.

Figure 17
:
Two representative diagrams with 1 perturbative Glauber and
N-1
nonperturbative Glauber gluons. In both diagrams, the perturbative Glauber
\tau_{p\neq 1}
attaches to the quark. The left panel and the right panel differ only by whether
\tau_{1}
attaches to the
q
or
\bar{q}
. These two diagrams sum to zero, as explained in eq. (
6.69
).

Vanishing of color-singlet exchange with
p=1
.

We now consider the
p=1
case, with the perturbative Glauber attached to the quark line,
which has the amplitude

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{N}^{\sigma_{1}}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{N_{q}-1}\bar{u}(k_{1})\frac{\not{n}}{2}\frac{(\not{k}_{1}-\not{\tau}_{\perp})}{(k_{1}-\tau)^{2}}\gamma^{\mu}{}

\displaystyle\times T^{A(\sigma_{1,q}^{N_{q}})}T^{A(\sigma_{1,q}^{N_{q}-1})}\cdots T^{A(\sigma_{1,q}^{2})}T^{A_{1}}T^{A({\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{1}})}\,T^{A(\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{2})}\cdots T^{A(\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{\mbox{\tiny$N_{\bar{q}}$}})}v(-k_{2})\,.

(6.70)

Swapping the position of the first Glauber no longer induces a cancellation as in eq. (
6.69
),
as the two diagrams have different Dirac structures, as in eq. (
6.47
).
We could instead consider swapping whether the final nonperturbative Glauber attachment attaches to the
q
or
\bar{q}
, which corresponds to a color structure in the second line of eq. (
6.3.1
) of either
T^{A(\sigma_{1,q}^{N_{q}})}=T^{A_{N}}
or
T^{A(\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{\mbox{\tiny$N_{\bar{q}}$}})}=T^{A_{N}}
.

\displaystyle T^{A_{N}}T^{A(\sigma_{1,q}^{N_{q}-1})}\cdots T^{A(\sigma_{1,q}^{2})}T^{A_{1}}T^{A({\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{1}})}\,T^{A(\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{2})}\cdots T^{A(\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{\mbox{\tiny$N_{\bar{q}}-1$}})}T^{A(\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{\mbox{\tiny$N_{\bar{q}}$}})}{}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle T^{A(\sigma_{1,q}^{N_{q}-1})}\cdots T^{A(\sigma_{1,q}^{2})}T^{A_{1}}T^{A({\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{1}})}\,T^{A(\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{2})}\cdots T^{A(\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{\mbox{\tiny$N_{\bar{q}}-1$}})}T^{A(\sigma_{1,\bar{q}}^{\mbox{\tiny$N_{\bar{q}}$}})}T^{A_{N}}\,,

(6.71)
